````math
The last group evaluates the selector, entity-to-document projection,
GFM pre-training, and supervised retrieval fine-tuning.

|  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|
| Reader Variant | HotpotQA |  | MuSiQue |  | 2WikiMultiHopQA |  |
|  | R@2 | R@5 | R@2 | R@5 | R@2 | R@5 |
| SAGE | 65.1 | 77.6 | 43.2 | 53.1 | 83.6 | 88.6 |
| Query planning and global addressing |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| SAGE w/o Structured Query Planning | 62.7 | 75.1 | 40.4 | 50.1 | 80.7 | 86.6 |
| SAGE w/o Global Soft Addressing | 59.3 | 72.5 | 37.6 | 47.4 | 75.9 | 83.1 |
| SAGE w/o Alias and Constraint Cues | 63.0 | 75.8 | 41.0 | 50.8 | 80.8 | 86.9 |
| SAGE w/ Anchor-only Initialization | 58.6 | 71.4 | 36.8 | 46.5 | 74.2 | 82.4 |
| Structurally conditioned propagation |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| SAGE w/o Structural Gate | 60.4 | 73.2 | 39.2 | 48.7 | 78.1 | 84.9 |
| SAGE w/o Node Structural Features | 62.1 | 74.8 | 40.5 | 50.0 | 80.0 | 86.0 |
| SAGE w/o Edge-pair Structural Features | 61.5 | 74.0 | 40.1 | 49.4 | 79.1 | 85.6 |
| SAGE w/o Graph-level Summary | 63.2 | 75.9 | 41.6 | 51.0 | 81.3 | 87.0 |
| SAGE w/ Uniform Message Passing | 58.9 | 71.8 | 37.5 | 46.9 | 75.3 | 82.9 |
| Cross-graph priors and target-graph calibration |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| SAGE w/o Schema Prior Channel | 62.4 | 75.0 | 40.9 | 50.6 | 80.4 | 86.4 |
| SAGE w/o Context Calibration Channel | 61.8 | 74.3 | 40.0 | 49.8 | 79.5 | 85.9 |
| SAGE w/o Context–Schema Fusion | 60.7 | 73.5 | 39.1 | 48.6 | 77.8 | 84.7 |
| Selector, projection, and reader training |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| SAGE w/o Controlled Entity-to-Document Projection | 60.9 | 73.9 | 38.7 | 48.2 | 77.2 | 84.4 |
| SAGE w/o Query-conditioned Selector | 63.9 | 76.5 | 41.9 | 52.0 | 82.2 | 87.6 |
| SAGE w/ Vanilla GNN Reader | 57.2 | 70.6 | 36.3 | 45.2 | 72.8 | 80.7 |

Table 8: Ablation study of the memory reader on multi-hop QA retrieval.
We report document-level Recall (%) at top-2 and top-5. All variants use
the same writer-produced graph memory unless otherwise specified.

The ablation results show the relative contribution of each
memory-reader component. First, structured query planning and global
soft addressing are important for fragmented-cue retrieval: removing
them noticeably weakens performance, especially on MuSiQue and
2WikiMultiHopQA, where evidence chains are more likely to depend on
implicit bridge entities. Second, structurally gated propagation
consistently improves over uniform message passing, indicating that
graph structure should be used selectively rather than as a fixed
expansion rule. Among the structural inputs, graph-level summaries have
a relatively smaller effect, while node-level and edge-pair features are
more important for recognizing hubs, bridges, and cross-community
evidence paths. Third, both the schema prior and context calibration
channels contribute to performance, suggesting that the reader benefits
from preserving transferable structural priors while adapting to the
current writer-produced graph. Finally, supervised retrieval fine-tuning
is essential for aligning the GFM reader with document-level evidence
retrieval, whereas GFM pre-training provides transferable structural
initialization that improves stability across datasets.

## Appendix I Implementation Details of the Query-conditioned Subgraph Selection Regularizer

We provide the implementation details of the query-conditioned subgraph
selector. In addition to the base entity scoring, it further learns a
soft gating probability $`\pi_{e}(q)`$, which characterizes whether
entity $`e`$ should enter the reading subgraph of the current query
$`q`$. This module performs lightweight reweighting of the final entity
score, and constrains the reading subgraph through several structural
regularizers during training.

##### Query-conditioned Selection Probability.

Given a query $`q`$ and a graph
$`\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})`$, let
$`\mathbf{h}_{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}`$ denote the representation of entity
node $`e\in\mathcal{V}`$ after propagation by the GFM backbone, and let
$`\mathbf{z}_{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}`$ denote the query representation
output by the query encoder and then linearly projected. Here, $`d`$ is
the hidden dimension. The selector first projects the node
representation and the query representation into the same selector
space:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbf{u}_{e}=W_{n}\mathbf{h}_{e},\qquad\mathbf{v}_{q}=W_{s}\mathbf{z}_{q},
``` |  | (177) |

where $`W_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{s}\times d}`$ is the node-side projection
matrix, $`W_{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{s}\times d}`$ is the query-side
projection matrix, and $`d_{s}`$ is the hidden dimension of the selector
space. In our implementation, we set $`d_{s}=d`$, but the two do not
have to be equal. Then, the selector obtains the selection logit of
entity $`e`$ with respect to query $`q`$ through a scaled inner product:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\zeta_{e}(q)=\frac{\mathbf{u}_{e}^{\top}\mathbf{v}_{q}}{T_{s}},
``` |  | (178) |

where $`T_{s}>0`$ is the selector temperature coefficient. The final
soft selection probability is defined as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\pi_{e}(q)=\operatorname{sigmoid}(\zeta_{e}(q))=\frac{1}{1+\exp(-\zeta_{e}(q))}.
``` |  | (179) |

Here, $`\pi_{e}(q)\in(0,1)`$ can be understood as the soft probability
that entity $`e`$ is included in the reading subgraph of the current
query. During training, we directly use $`\pi_{e}(q)`$ for
differentiable optimization; during inference, we can either continue to
use the soft probability for reweighting, or obtain a discrete subgraph
according to a threshold $`\tau_{\pi}`$:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{V}_{q}=\{e\in\mathcal{V}\mid\pi_{e}(q)>\tau_{\pi}\},\qquad\mathcal{E}_{q}=\{(u,v)\in\mathcal{E}\mid u,v\in\mathcal{V}_{q}\}.
``` |  | (180) |

Let $`a_{e}(q)`$ denote the base entity score given by the GFM backbone
reader. This score is usually obtained from the similarity between the
node representation and the query representation, for example

|     |                                               |     |       |
|-----|-----------------------------------------------|-----|-------|
|     |
       ``` math
       a_{e}(q)=\mathbf{h}_{e}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{q}.
       ```                                            |     | (181) |

Finally, we have:

|     |                                                             |     |       |
|-----|-------------------------------------------------------------|-----|-------|
|     |
       ``` math
       a_{e}^{\mathrm{final}}(q)=a_{e}(q)+\lambda_{s}\zeta_{e}(q),
       ```                                                          |     | (182) |

where $`\lambda_{s}\geq 0`$ controls the influence of the selector logit
on the final entity ranking.

##### Query–Subgraph Contrastive Regularizer.

Using only
Eq. ([182](#A9.E182 "Equation 182 ‣ Query-conditioned Selection Probability. ‣ Appendix I Implementation Details of the Query-conditioned Subgraph Selection Regularizer ‣ SAGE: A Self-Evolving Agentic Graph-Memory Engine for Structure-AwareAssociative Memory"))
to fuse the selector score can easily lead to two types of degeneration:
first, the selector may assign high probabilities to most nodes, thereby
degenerating into full-graph activation; second, the selector may only
learn local high-frequency entities, without forming a subgraph
representation that is consistent with the overall semantics of the
query. To this end, we first construct a query-conditioned subgraph
representation weighted by the selection probabilities:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\bar{\mathbf{h}}_{\pi}(q)=\frac{\sum_{e\in\mathcal{V}}\pi_{e}(q)\mathbf{h}_{e}}{\sum_{e\in\mathcal{V}}\pi_{e}(q)+\epsilon},
``` |  | (183) |

where $`\epsilon>0`$ is a numerical stability term, which avoids an
excessively small denominator when all $`\pi_{e}(q)`$ are close to
$`0`$. $`\bar{\mathbf{h}}_{\pi}(q)`$ can be understood as the semantic
center of the soft subgraph activated by the current selector.

For a mini-batch $`\mathcal{B}=\{q_{i}\}_{i=1}^{B}`$, we treat
$`(\bar{\mathbf{h}}_{\pi}(q_{i}),\mathbf{z}_{q_{i}})`$ within the same
sample as a positive pair, and treat
$`(\bar{\mathbf{h}}_{\pi}(q_{i}),\mathbf{z}_{q_{j}})`$, $`j\neq i`$, as
in-batch negative pairs. The query–subgraph contrastive loss is defined
as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Omega_{\mathrm{nce}}=-\frac{1}{B}\sum_{i=1}^{B}\log\frac{\exp\left(\mathrm{sim}(\bar{\mathbf{h}}_{\pi}(q_{i}),\mathbf{z}_{q_{i}})/T_{n}\right)}{\sum_{j=1}^{B}\exp\left(\mathrm{sim}(\bar{\mathbf{h}}_{\pi}(q_{i}),\mathbf{z}_{q_{j}})/T_{n}\right)},
``` |  | (184) |

where $`T_{n}>0`$ is the contrastive learning temperature coefficient,
and $`\mathrm{sim}(\cdot,\cdot)`$ is the similarity function. In
implementation, we usually apply $`\ell_{2}`$ normalization to
$`\bar{\mathbf{h}}_{\pi}(q)`$ and $`\mathbf{z}_{q}`$, and use
inner-product similarity, so that
$`\mathrm{sim}(\bar{\mathbf{h}}_{\pi}(q),\mathbf{z}_{q})=\frac{\bar{\mathbf{h}}_{\pi}(q)^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{q}}{\|\bar{\mathbf{h}}_{\pi}(q)\|_{2}\|\mathbf{z}_{q}\|_{2}}`$.
This term encourages the soft subgraph activated by the selector to
semantically represent the current query, rather than only selecting
nodes with high frequency or high centrality in the graph.

##### Size Regularizer.

To prevent the selector from improving recall by activating a large
number of nodes, we use the average selection probability as a size
penalty:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Omega_{\mathrm{size}}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{V}|}\sum_{e\in\mathcal{V}}\pi_{e}(q).
``` |  | (185) |

This term approximately represents the expected proportion of activated
nodes. Minimizing $`\Omega_{\mathrm{size}}`$ pushes the model to select
a smaller reading subgraph. However, this term cannot be used alone;
otherwise, the selector may degenerate into selecting too few nodes or
even no nodes. Therefore, it needs to be jointly optimized with
$`\Omega_{\mathrm{nce}}`$ and the main retrieval loss: the former
ensures query relevance, while the latter ensures that the selected
structure can still support correct entity and document recall.

##### Connectivity Smoothing Regularizer.

In addition to controllable size, an effective reading subgraph should
also have local structural coherence. If the selection probabilities of
adjacent nodes differ too much, the model may form several isolated
activated points, making multi-hop paths difficult to explicitly
utilize. To this end, we use a smoothing penalty on edges:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Omega_{\mathrm{con}}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}|}\sum_{(u,v)\in\mathcal{E}}\left(\pi_{u}(q)-\pi_{v}(q)\right)^{2}.
``` |  | (186) |

Here, $`(u,v)`$ is a directed or undirected edge in the graph, depending
on whether edge directions are preserved during graph construction. If
an undirected graph is used, $`\mathcal{E}`$ can be viewed as the
symmetrized edge set. This term does not force all selected nodes to be
strictly connected, but encourages adjacent nodes to have similar
selection probabilities. In matrix form, if
$`\bm{\pi}(q)\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{V}|}`$ is the selection
probability vector composed of $`\pi_{e}(q)`$, and $`\mathbf{L}`$ is the
graph Laplacian matrix, then this term is equivalent to Laplacian
smoothing:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\Omega_{\mathrm{con}}\propto\bm{\pi}(q)^{\top}\mathbf{L}\bm{\pi}(q).
``` |  | (187) |

Therefore, it is consistent with the classical assumption of graph
signal smoothing: query relevance, as a soft signal on the graph, should
maintain a certain degree of continuity within local neighborhoods.

##### Computational Complexity of the Selector Itself.

Let the batch size be $`B`$, the number of nodes be $`n=|\mathcal{V}|`$,
the number of edges be $`m=|\mathcal{E}|`$, and the hidden dimension be
$`d`$. The cost of computing $`W_{n}\mathbf{h}_{e}`$ is $`O(Bnd^{2})`$,
the cost of computing $`W_{s}\mathbf{z}_{q}`$ is $`O(Bd^{2})`$, and the
cost of the inner-product logits is $`O(Bnd)`$. If we cache
$`W_{n}\mathbf{h}_{e}`$ in advance, this term can be reduced to
$`O(Bnd)`$. For the contrastive term, the cost of the subgraph pooling
in
Eq. ([183](#A9.E183 "Equation 183 ‣ Query–Subgraph Contrastive Regularizer. ‣ Appendix I Implementation Details of the Query-conditioned Subgraph Selection Regularizer ‣ SAGE: A Self-Evolving Agentic Graph-Memory Engine for Structure-AwareAssociative Memory"))
is $`O(Bnd)`$, and the cost of the in-batch NCE similarity matrix is
$`O(B^{2}d)`$; the size term has cost $`O(Bn)`$; the connectivity term
needs to traverse edges and has cost $`O(Bm)`$. Therefore, the
additional complexity of the selector during training is

|     |                                      |     |       |
|-----|--------------------------------------|-----|-------|
|     |
       ``` math
       O\left(Bnd^{2}+Bnd+B^{2}d+Bm\right),
       ```                                   |     | (188) |

and if the quadratic term of the linear projection is ignored or cached,
it can be approximated as

|     |                              |     |       |
|-----|------------------------------|-----|-------|
|     |
       ``` math
       O\left(Bnd+B^{2}d+Bm\right).
       ```                           |     | (189) |

During inference, if only the selector logit is used to fuse entity
scores, without computing NCE, size, and connectivity regularizers, then
the additional cost is mainly $`O(Bnd^{2}+Bnd)`$, or $`O(Bnd)`$ under
caching/lightweight projection.

## Appendix J Training and Inference Complexity

This appendix separates three notions of cost that are easy to conflate:
(i) the asymptotic complexity of the SAGE graph reader, (ii) one-time
offline costs for graph construction, indexing, and training, and (iii)
the wall-clock and LM-call cost of a fully uncached online query. This
distinction is important because the retrieval-engine times in
Table [4](#S5.T4 "Table 4 ‣ Domain-specific Memory ‣ 5.1 End-to-End Effectiveness ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ SAGE: A Self-Evolving Agentic Graph-Memory Engine for Structure-AwareAssociative Memory")
measure the retriever-side stage rather than the complete agent
pipeline. The best HotpotQA/2WikiMultiHopQA/MuSiQue evaluation runs also
used precomputed query-planning/entity-linking artifacts, whereas a
fresh online query must execute these stages explicitly.

Unless otherwise stated, the measured costs below come from the same
shared server used for our experiments, equipped with $`8\times`$ NVIDIA
A100-SXM4-80GB GPUs. We distinguish values recovered directly from logs,
controlled latency measurements, and formula-based estimates. We do not
fill in missing historical measurements with estimates from a different
run.

### J.1 End-to-End Query Pipeline and Accounting Boundary

A fully uncached query follows the pipeline

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle q`$ | $`\displaystyle\rightarrow\text{planner extractor}\rightarrow\text{planner inferer}\rightarrow\text{LLM NER}`$ |  | (190) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\rightarrow\text{local entity linking}\rightarrow\text{query embedding}\rightarrow\text{GFM retrieval/document ranking}\rightarrow\text{answer generation}.`$ |  |  |

The query planner uses two serial LM calls. The extractor identifies
explicit entities, candidate aliases, relation clues, hard constraints,
and the answer type. The inferer consumes the extractor output and
generates at most three pseudo-queries together with confidence scores.
The pipeline then applies one additional LM call for NER. Entity linking
is performed locally with colbert-ir/colbertv2.0; query encoding, graph
retrieval, and document ranking are also local computations. Finally,
the top retrieved documents are passed to the answer model, which
contributes one LM call.

Therefore, in the normal successful uncached path, the online system
uses four LM calls per question:

|                                 |                  |
|---------------------------------|------------------|
| Stage                           | LM calls / query |
| Planner extractor               | 1                |
| Planner inferer                 | 1                |
| LLM NER                         | 1                |
| Local ColBERT entity linking    | 0                |
| Query embedding + GFM retrieval | 0                |
| Answer generation               | 1                |
| Total                           | 4                |

Table 9: LM calls in one successful fully uncached online query. Entity
linking and graph retrieval are local and do not invoke a remote LM.

##### Cached benchmark evaluation versus uncached deployment.

The best HotpotQA, 2WikiMultiHopQA, and MuSiQue Stage-3 evaluations did
not invoke the planner and NER from scratch inside Stage 3. Instead, the
pipeline first exported a rewriter.jsonl file containing the planned
explicit entities, pseudo-queries, and linked entities, and Stage 3
reused these artifacts. Consequently, the logged Stage-3 wall-clock
contains local query encoding, graph retrieval, document ranking, answer
generation, retries, and evaluation, but excludes the two planner calls,
the LLM-NER call, and online entity linking. We therefore report cached
evaluation time and fully uncached online latency separately.

##### Scope of Table [4](#S5.T4 "Table 4 ‣ Domain-specific Memory ‣ 5.1 End-to-End Effectiveness ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ SAGE: A Self-Evolving Agentic Graph-Memory Engine for Structure-AwareAssociative Memory").

The values in
Table [4](#S5.T4 "Table 4 ‣ Domain-specific Memory ‣ 5.1 End-to-End Effectiveness ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ SAGE: A Self-Evolving Agentic Graph-Memory Engine for Structure-AwareAssociative Memory")
should be interpreted as *retrieval-engine* measurements, not as
end-to-end agent latency. In particular, they should not be compared
directly with the uncached latency numbers below, which additionally
account for query planning, NER, entity linking, and answer generation.

### J.2 Notation

For the asymptotic analysis, suppose that the graph
$`\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})`$ contains $`n=|\mathcal{V}|`$
entity nodes and $`m=|\mathcal{E}|`$ entity-relation edges. If
self-loops are added during GCN propagation, we denote
$`\tilde{m}=m+n`$. Let the hidden dimension be $`d`$, the number of
propagation layers be $`L`$, the batch size be $`B`$, and the number of
pseudo-queries be $`M`$. One original query together with $`M`$
pseudo-queries therefore requires at most $`M+1`$ graph reads.

Let $`\mathbf{M}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times N_{D}}`$ denote the sparse
entity–document association matrix, where $`N_{D}`$ is the number of
documents and $`\operatorname{nnz}(\mathbf{M})`$ is the number of
nonzero entity–document links. Let $`K_{e}`$ be the number of top
entities used for document projection, $`K`$ the number of final
retrieved documents, and $`\bar{f}`$ the average number of documents
linked to the selected entities.

### J.3 Offline Structural Features and Indexing

Let the dimensions of node-, edge-, and graph-level structural features
be $`p_{n}`$, $`p_{e}`$, and $`p_{g}`$, respectively. In our
implementation these are small constants.

Given an adjacency list, degrees and average neighbor degrees can be
computed in $`O(n+m)`$ time. Clustering coefficients and common-neighbor
features require neighbor-set intersections, with cost

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
O\left(n+m+\sum_{(u,v)\in\mathcal{E}}\min\{\deg(u),\deg(v)\}\right).
``` |  | (191) |

For sparse graphs or graphs with bounded average degree,
Eq. ([191](#A10.E191 "Equation 191 ‣ J.3 Offline Structural Features and Indexing ‣ Appendix J Training and Inference Complexity ‣ SAGE: A Self-Evolving Agentic Graph-Memory Engine for Structure-AwareAssociative Memory"))
is close to $`O(n+m)`$, while an extremely dense worst case can reach
$`O(n^{3})`$. The structural features and the entity–document matrix can
be precomputed and cached, requiring

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
O\left(np_{n}+mp_{e}+p_{g}+\operatorname{nnz}(\mathbf{M})\right)
``` |  | (192) |

space. Because these quantities are query-independent, they can be
reused across queries and across repeated reader evaluations of the same
candidate graph in the self-evolution loop.

### J.4 Forward Complexity of the Structurally Gated GFM

We first consider one forward pass for one query on one graph. A
standard GCN layer consists of a node-wise linear transformation and
sparse adjacency aggregation. The corresponding cost is

|     |                                                     |     |       |
|-----|-----------------------------------------------------|-----|-------|
|     |
       ``` math
       C_{\mathrm{plain}}=O\left(nd^{2}+\tilde{m}d\right).
       ```                                                  |     | (193) |

A structurally gated layer additionally generates a vector gate for each
edge:

|     |                                                            |     |       |
|-----|------------------------------------------------------------|-----|-------|
|     |
       ``` math
       \mathbf{m}_{u\rightarrow v}=\gg_{uv}\odot W\mathbf{h}_{u},
       ```                                                         |     | (194) |

where $`\mathbf{h}_{u}`$ is the source-node representation,
$`W\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}`$ is the node transformation,
$`\gg_{uv}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}`$ is the structural gate for edge $`(u,v)`$,
and $`\odot`$ is element-wise multiplication. Let $`d_{g}`$ denote the
structural-feature encoding dimension and $`h_{g}`$ the hidden width of
the gating MLP. When the gate uses source-node structure, target-node
structure, edge-pair structure, and a graph-level summary, its
generation cost is

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{gate}}=O\Big(`$ | $`\displaystyle np_{n}d_{g}+mp_{e}d_{g}+p_{g}d_{g}`$ |  | (195) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle+m(4d_{g}h_{g}+h_{g}d)\Big).`$ |  |  |

The first three terms encode node, edge, and graph structural features;
the last term is the per-edge gating MLP. If edge-pair features or graph
summaries are disabled, their terms can be removed. If $`d_{g}`$ and
$`h_{g}`$ scale with $`d`$, gate generation is $`O(md^{2})`$ in the
worst case. If the gate is a small constant-width or low-rank module,
its practical scaling approaches $`O(md)`$. A structurally gated layer
therefore costs

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
C_{\mathrm{gated}}=O\left(nd^{2}+\tilde{m}d+C_{\mathrm{gate}}\right).
``` |  | (196) |

The implementation can combine a holistic structurally gated branch with
a specific prompt branch. Let
$`\rho_{\mathrm{plain}},\rho_{\mathrm{gated}}\in\{0,1\}`$ indicate
whether the corresponding branches are enabled. For a batch of $`B`$
queries,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
C_{\mathrm{enc}}(B)=O\left(BL\left[\rho_{\mathrm{plain}}C_{\mathrm{plain}}+\rho_{\mathrm{gated}}C_{\mathrm{gated}}\right]\right).
``` |  | (197) |

The factor $`B`$ appears because the current implementation constructs
query-conditioned node inputs and performs graph encoding for each
query. Caching query-independent structural features reduces constants
but does not change the conservative upper bound in
Eq. ([197](#A10.E197 "Equation 197 ‣ J.4 Forward Complexity of the Structurally Gated GFM ‣ Appendix J Training and Inference Complexity ‣ SAGE: A Self-Evolving Agentic Graph-Memory Engine for Structure-AwareAssociative Memory")).

For the simplified setting used in the main-text complexity discussion,
$`B=1`$, $`M=0`$, only one branch is active, and the gating network is
treated as lightweight.
Equation ([197](#A10.E197 "Equation 197 ‣ J.4 Forward Complexity of the Structurally Gated GFM ‣ Appendix J Training and Inference Complexity ‣ SAGE: A Self-Evolving Agentic Graph-Memory Engine for Structure-AwareAssociative Memory"))
then reduces to

|     |                             |     |       |
|-----|-----------------------------|-----|-------|
|     |
       ``` math
       O\left(L(md+d^{2}n)\right),
       ```                          |     | (198) |

where $`md`$ represents sparse edge-level message propagation/gating and
$`d^{2}n`$ the node-wise linear projections.

### J.5 Entity Scoring, Selector Regularization, and Document Projection

After graph encoding, the entity score is

|     |                                               |     |       |
|-----|-----------------------------------------------|-----|-------|
|     |
       ``` math
       a_{e}(q)=\mathbf{h}_{e}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{q}.
       ```                                            |     | (199) |

For a batch of $`B`$ queries, scoring all $`n`$ entities costs

|     |                               |     |       |
|-----|-------------------------------|-----|-------|
|     |
       ``` math
       C_{\mathrm{score}}(B)=O(Bnd).
       ```                            |     | (200) |

If the query-conditioned subgraph selector in
Appendix [I](#A9 "Appendix I Implementation Details of the Query-conditioned Subgraph Selection Regularizer ‣ SAGE: A Self-Evolving Agentic Graph-Memory Engine for Structure-AwareAssociative Memory")
is enabled, its inference-stage overhead is

|     |                                           |     |       |
|-----|-------------------------------------------|-----|-------|
|     |
       ``` math
       C_{\mathrm{sel,infer}}(B)=O(Bnd^{2}+Bnd),
       ```                                        |     | (201) |

and can approach $`O(Bnd)`$ when the node-side projection is cached or a
lightweight projection is used. During training, the NCE, size, and
connectivity regularizers add

|     |                                                     |     |       |
|-----|-----------------------------------------------------|-----|-------|
|     |
       ``` math
       C_{\mathrm{sel,train}}(B)=O(Bnd^{2}+Bnd+B^{2}d+Bm),
       ```                                                  |     | (202) |

where $`B^{2}d`$ is the in-batch query–subgraph contrastive term and
$`Bm`$ corresponds to edge-level connectivity smoothing.

Entity scores are projected to documents through the sparse association
matrix $`\mathbf{M}`$. Full sparse matrix multiplication costs

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
C_{\mathrm{doc,full}}(B)=O\left(B\operatorname{nnz}(\mathbf{M})\right).
``` |  | (203) |

The current IDFWeightedRanker follows this sparse-projection form. If
only the top $`K_{e}`$ entities are projected through inverted lists,
the conceptual cost is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
C_{\mathrm{doc,top}K}(B)=O\left(Bn\log K_{e}+BK_{e}\bar{f}\right),
``` |  | (204) |

where the first term selects top entities and the second accesses their
linked documents. Final top-$`K`$ ranking over all $`N_{D}`$ documents
costs $`O(BN_{D}\log K)`$; ranking only a candidate pool costs
$`O(BN_{\mathrm{cand}}\log K)`$, where $`N_{\mathrm{cand}}\ll N_{D}`$.

### J.6 Training-Time Complexity

The reader-training cost is dominated by GFM forward propagation,
entity-level retrieval loss, the optional selector regularizer, and
backpropagation. Let $`\kappa_{\mathrm{bw}}`$ denote the constant-factor
cost of backpropagation relative to the forward computation. For BCE,
ranking loss, or ListCE over all entity scores,

|     |                             |     |       |
|-----|-----------------------------|-----|-------|
|     |
       ``` math
       C_{\mathrm{loss}}(B)=O(Bn).
       ```                          |     | (205) |

Without the optional selector,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
C_{\mathrm{train}}=O\left(\kappa_{\mathrm{bw}}\left[C_{\mathrm{enc}}(B)+C_{\mathrm{score}}(B)+C_{\mathrm{loss}}(B)\right]\right).
``` |  | (206) |

With the selector,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
C_{\mathrm{train}}^{\mathrm{sel}}=O\left(\kappa_{\mathrm{bw}}\left[C_{\mathrm{enc}}(B)+C_{\mathrm{score}}(B)+C_{\mathrm{loss}}(B)+C_{\mathrm{sel,train}}(B)\right]\right).
``` |  | (207) |

If document-level supervision is added explicitly, document projection
adds either $`C_{\mathrm{doc,full}}(B)`$ or
$`C_{\mathrm{doc,top}K}(B)`$.

The GFM pretraining/fine-tuning computation itself uses cached graph,
entity–document, and embedding artifacts and therefore does not require
remote LM calls during each optimization step. LM use in raw graph
construction is a separate one-time preprocessing cost quantified below.

### J.7 Inference-Time Complexity

Let $`C_{\mathrm{prep}}(q)`$ denote the query-preparation cost before
graph reading, including query planning, NER, entity linking, and query
encoding. The graph-reader portion for one original query and $`M`$
pseudo-queries is

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{reader}}(q)=`$ | $`\displaystyle(M+1)\Big[C_{\mathrm{enc}}(1)+C_{\mathrm{score}}(1)+C_{\mathrm{sel,infer}}(1)+C_{\mathrm{doc}}(1)\Big]`$ |  | (208) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle+C_{\mathrm{fuse}}(M,K),`$ |  |  |

where $`C_{\mathrm{doc}}(1)`$ is either full sparse projection or
top-$`K_{e}`$ inverted projection; $`C_{\mathrm{sel,infer}}(1)=0`$ when
the optional selector is disabled. If every query retains $`K`$
candidate documents, simple weighted fusion costs

|     |                                                          |     |       |
|-----|----------------------------------------------------------|-----|-------|
|     |
       ``` math
       C_{\mathrm{fuse}}(M,K)=O\left((M+1)K\log((M+1)K)\right).
       ```                                                       |     | (209) |

The complete uncached online cost additionally includes query
preparation and answer generation:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
C_{\mathrm{online}}(q)=C_{\mathrm{prep}}(q)+C_{\mathrm{reader}}(q)+C_{\mathrm{answer}}(q).
``` |  | (210) |

This decomposition is the one used in the latency audit below. In
particular, the small graph-reader latency reported in
Table [4](#S5.T4 "Table 4 ‣ Domain-specific Memory ‣ 5.1 End-to-End Effectiveness ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ SAGE: A Self-Evolving Agentic Graph-Memory Engine for Structure-AwareAssociative Memory")
should not be substituted for $`C_{\mathrm{online}}(q)`$.

### J.8 Space Complexity

Offline graph storage requires

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
O\left(n+m+\operatorname{nnz}(\mathbf{M})+np_{n}+mp_{e}+p_{g}\right).
``` |  | (211) |

During training, storing layer-wise node activations requires

|     |          |     |       |
|-----|----------|-----|-------|
|     |
       ``` math
       O(BLnd)
       ```       |     | (212) |

space. Fully materializing all edge gates/messages would require
$`O(md)`$ GPU memory. The implementation instead streams edges in
chunks. For chunk size $`c`$, the peak edge-message memory becomes

|     |                      |     |       |
|-----|----------------------|-----|-------|
|     |
       ``` math
       O(cd),\qquad c\ll m.
       ```                   |     | (213) |

If return_gate is enabled and all edge gates are retained for
visualization, memory returns to $`O(md)`$. Materializing all document
scores requires $`O(BN_{D})`$ space, while maintaining only a candidate
heap can reduce this to $`O(BK)`$ or $`O(BK_{e}\bar{f})`$.

### J.9 Measured Training Compute

##### Audited pretraining checkpoint.

The training server contains eight A100-SXM4-80GB GPUs, but the
auditable pretraining run used three A100-80GB GPUs. The corresponding
configuration uses a 6-layer GCN backbone with hidden dimension 256,
GraphCL pretraining, 128 negative samples, Adam with learning rate
$`5\times 10^{-4}`$, activation checkpointing, and 273 training shards.
The epoch-97 checkpoint contains 22,233,918 parameters.

The run is recoverable from two logged segments. The first segment ran
for 18 h 26 min 43 s on three GPUs, corresponding to approximately 55.34
GPU-hours. The second resumed from epoch 94 and recomputed through epoch
97, running for 46 min 17 s, or approximately 2.31 GPU-hours. Counting
the recomputed work, the total auditable cost to the epoch-97 checkpoint
is therefore approximately

|     |                         |     |       |
|-----|-------------------------|-----|-------|
|     |
       ``` math
       57.65\ \text{GPU-hours}
       ```                      |     | (214) |

and 19 h 13 min of aggregate wall-clock training time on three GPUs.

|                           |                         |
|---------------------------|-------------------------|
| Item                      | Value                   |
| Hardware used by run      | $`3\times`$ A100-80GB   |
| Backbone                  | 6-layer GCN             |
| Hidden dimension          | 256                     |
| Training shards           | 273                     |
| Checkpoint parameters     | 22,233,918              |
| Logged wall-clock compute | $`\approx 19`$ h 13 min |
| Logged GPU-hours          | $`\approx 57.65`$       |

Table 10: Audited compute for the recoverable epoch-97 GFM pretraining
checkpoint. GPU-hours include the resumed segment and its recomputation.

##### Limitation of the historical fine-tuning record.

The historical directory corresponding to the fine-tuning run that
produced the best reported HotpotQA/2WikiMultiHopQA/MuSiQue checkpoint
is no longer available. Its exact wall-clock time and peak-memory trace
therefore cannot be reconstructed. We intentionally do not substitute
the duration of a different fine-tuning attempt and present it as the
cost of the best model. The 57.65 GPU-hour number above should
consequently be interpreted as the auditable pretraining cost, not as a
claim about the complete historical end-to-end training budget.

##### Configuration-matched fine-tuning reference.

For scale, a later fine-tuning attempt with the closest recoverable
configuration used five A100-80GB GPUs, batch size 1, gradient
accumulation 4, 100 batches per epoch, and the same 273 training shards.
Its first epoch, including validation and checkpointing, took
approximately 3 h 20 min, or 16.68 GPU-hours. That run was terminated
during epoch 2 and did not produce the best reported checkpoint. We
therefore report this number only as a configuration-level reference and
do not extrapolate it into an “actual” fine-tuning cost for the final
model.

### J.10 Offline LM Cost for Raw Graph Construction

Raw Stage-1 graph construction performs one LLM-NER call and one OpenIE
call per document, and one LLM-NER call per QA question. If no processed
artifact is cached, the one-time LM-call count is therefore

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
N_{\mathrm{LM,Stage1}}=2N_{\mathrm{doc}}+N_{\mathrm{question}}.
``` |  | (215) |

For the 273 multi-hop training shards used in the audit, the raw
instance counts imply the call budget in
Table [11](#A10.T11 "Table 11 ‣ J.10 Offline LM Cost for Raw Graph Construction ‣ Appendix J Training and Inference Complexity ‣ SAGE: A Self-Evolving Agentic Graph-Memory Engine for Structure-AwareAssociative Memory").
These are *rebuild estimates*: the audited GFM training run reused
processed artifacts and did not regenerate all of these calls.

|                 |        |                    |              |                  |
|-----------------|--------|--------------------|--------------|------------------|
| Dataset         | Shards | Document instances | QA questions | Stage-1 LM calls |
| 2WikiMultiHopQA | 168    | 966,907            | 167,454      | 2,101,268        |
| HotpotQA        | 89     | 864,849            | 88,447       | 1,818,145        |
| MuSiQue         | 16     | 318,724            | 15,938       | 653,386          |
| Total           | 273    | 2,150,480          | 271,839      | 4,572,799        |

Table 11: One-time Stage-1 LM-call estimate if the 273 training shards
are rebuilt from raw data without cached preprocessing. Document counts
are raw document instances before cross-shard deduplication.

The 2,150,480 document instances contain substantial duplication across
shards. Normalizing and deduplicating by title–content pairs leaves
1,101,879 unique pairs. If such global deduplication is performed before
both document-level LM calls, the estimated Stage-1 budget decreases by

|     |                                              |     |       |
|-----|----------------------------------------------|-----|-------|
|     |
       ``` math
       2(2{,}150{,}480-1{,}101{,}879)=2{,}097{,}202
       ```                                           |     | (216) |

calls, from 4,572,799 to 2,475,597 calls, a 45.9% reduction. This
identifies cross-shard deduplication as a direct optimization for future
raw rebuilds.

For reference, rebuilding the three 1,000-question evaluation corpora
from raw data would require 19,442 Stage-1 calls for HotpotQA
($`9{,}221`$ documents), 13,238 for 2WikiMultiHopQA ($`6{,}119`$
documents), and 24,312 for MuSiQue ($`11{,}656`$ documents). Again,
these are one-time raw-rebuild costs rather than costs repeated for each
evaluation.

### J.11 Online LM Calls and Token Footprint

Under the successful uncached path in
Table [9](#A10.T9 "Table 9 ‣ J.1 End-to-End Query Pipeline and Accounting Boundary ‣ Appendix J Training and Inference Complexity ‣ SAGE: A Self-Evolving Agentic Graph-Memory Engine for Structure-AwareAssociative Memory"),
1,000 queries require 4,000 successful LM calls: 1,000 planner-extractor
calls, 1,000 planner-inferer calls, 1,000 NER calls, and 1,000
answer-generation calls.

We additionally audited the prompt/response content that remained
available from the multi-hop evaluation pipeline. The answer prompts and
responses can be reconstructed directly from saved predictions. Planner
outputs are only partially preserved, and raw NER outputs are not fully
preserved; therefore, planner/NER token counts are lower-bound/proxy
estimates rather than provider billing records. Message-framing tokens
are not included.

|  |  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|
| Dataset | Planner extractor input | Planner inferer input | NER input | Answer input | Answer output |
| HotpotQA | 106 | 124 | 82 | 2,234 | 105 |
| 2WikiMultiHopQA | 102 | 120 | 78 | 1,949 | 87 |
| MuSiQue | 107 | 124 | 83 | 2,013 | 100 |

Table 12: Audited per-question token footprint. Planner-inferer input is
a lower bound because not all intermediate planner fields are retained.
The answer input contains the retrieved top-10 document context.

The answer context dominates the recoverable input-token footprint.
Across the three datasets, the top-10 answer prompt accounts for
approximately 86–88% of the audited input tokens. For roughly 1,000
successful queries, the lower-bound four-call input totals are 2.545M
tokens for HotpotQA, 2.248M for 2WikiMultiHopQA, and 2.327M for MuSiQue;
the corresponding recoverable output-token proxies are 0.296M, 0.267M,
and 0.288M, respectively. We report token volumes instead of a fixed
dollar figure because provider pricing can change over time.

##### Retries in the historical benchmark runs.

The Stage-3 answer wrapper allowed up to five attempts and slept for 30
s after an error. The saved logs contain 260 API-error events for
HotpotQA, 223 for 2WikiMultiHopQA, and 156 for MuSiQue. With 999, 999,
and 1,000 saved answers, respectively, the logs imply at least 1,259,
1,222, and 1,156 answer request attempts. The dominant recorded errors
were HTTP 429 rate-limit events while the three evaluations were running
concurrently under a shared API project. These retries affect wall-clock
time, but rejected requests should not automatically be interpreted as
successful billed generations.

### J.12 Measured Stage-Wise Online Latency

We performed a controlled latency audit using the first 20 saved
HotpotQA queries on the same shared server. Query planning was measured
on 20 examples (two serial LM calls per example), LLM NER on 20
examples, and local entity linking on 20 examples. Answer generation was
measured on the first 10 examples using the original top-10
retrieved-document context. The entity-linking test used an otherwise
available A100 GPU and searched over 87,768 HotpotQA graph entities.
Because the server was shared and under nontrivial load, these values
describe the measured environment rather than an ideal dedicated
deployment.

|                             |       |          |            |         |
|-----------------------------|-------|----------|------------|---------|
| Stage                       | $`N`$ | Mean (s) | Median (s) | P95 (s) |
| Planner (2 serial LM calls) | 20    | 4.72     | 4.40       | 12.93   |
| LLM NER                     | 20    | 1.52     | 1.30       | 2.63    |
| Entity linking              | 20    | 3.08     | 2.25       | 14.30   |
| Answer generation           | 10    | 5.55     | 5.27       | 10.22   |

Table 13: Measured latency of audited online stages. The graph
query-encoding/GFM/document-ranking stage was not separately timed in
this controlled audit and is therefore not included in the 13.22 s sum
below.

The two planner calls are necessarily serial in the current
implementation: the inferer consumes the extractor JSON, so they cannot
be issued in parallel without changing the planner. The measured median
of the directly audited stages is

|     |                                      |     |       |
|-----|--------------------------------------|-----|-------|
|     |
       ``` math
       4.40+1.30+2.25+5.27=13.22\ \text{s}.
       ```                                   |     | (217) |

This is *not* the full end-to-end median. The controlled audit did not
separately measure Qwen3-Embedding-8B query encoding, GFM forward passes
for the primary/pseudo queries, document ranking, or retrieval-result
fusion. The only supported statement from these measurements is
therefore

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
T_{\mathrm{uncached,online}}>13.22\ \text{s}\quad\text{(median in the audited environment).}
``` |  | (218) |

##### Entity-linking initialization.

Each query supplied a mean of 7.85 mentions to ColBERT (median $`7`$,
P95 $`14`$), with top-5 linked entities per mention. The first
construction of the HotpotQA entity index required 10.42 s to load the
ColBERT model and 441.65 s to build the index. When an existing index
was reused, model loading took 3.87 s and index reuse 2.82 s. The first
searcher query also loads search structures and CUDA components,
explaining the 14.30 s P95/cold-query value in
Table [13](#A10.T13 "Table 13 ‣ J.12 Measured Stage-Wise Online Latency ‣ Appendix J Training and Inference Complexity ‣ SAGE: A Self-Evolving Agentic Graph-Memory Engine for Structure-AwareAssociative Memory").
Index construction is an offline startup cost, and a deployed service
should build/reuse and warm the index before serving online queries
rather than charge this cost to every request.

### J.13 Observed Stage-3 Evaluation Wall-Clock

For completeness,
Table [14](#A10.T14 "Table 14 ‣ J.13 Observed Stage-3 Evaluation Wall-Clock ‣ Appendix J Training and Inference Complexity ‣ SAGE: A Self-Evolving Agentic Graph-Memory Engine for Structure-AwareAssociative Memory")
reports the wall-clock of the historical best Stage-3 multi-hop
evaluations. Each run contains 1,000 questions, uses n_threads=5 for
concurrent answer calls, supplies top-10 documents to the answer model,
and reuses precomputed planner/NER/entity-linking results.

|                 |                 |                       |                  |
|-----------------|-----------------|-----------------------|------------------|
| Dataset         | Wall-clock      | Wall-clock / question | Throughput (q/s) |
| HotpotQA        | 2 h 30 min 45 s | 9.05 s                | 0.110            |
| 2WikiMultiHopQA | 2 h 00 min 24 s | 7.23 s                | 0.138            |
| MuSiQue         | 2 h 30 min 40 s | 9.04 s                | 0.111            |

Table 14: Observed Stage-3 evaluation wall-clock. These times are not
single-query uncached latencies: planner/NER/entity linking are
precomputed, five answer calls can run concurrently, and the runs
include rate-limit retries and evaluation overhead.

These Stage-3 runs include model/data initialization, local query
embedding, primary and pseudo-query graph retrieval, document ranking,
five-thread answer generation, retry sleeps, and the final evaluator.
They exclude the precomputed two-call planner, LLM NER, and online
entity-linking stages. Moreover, the three datasets were evaluated
concurrently and shared the same API project’s rate limit, so these
wall-clock values should not be interpreted as isolated single-user
latency.

### J.14 Complexity Interpretation and Comparison

Different retrieval architectures place their cost in different parts of
the pipeline. Dense single-step RAG primarily pays for query encoding
and nearest- neighbor search over a prebuilt index. Iterative RAG adds
repeated retrieval and, when generation/reasoning is interleaved,
repeated LM calls. GraphRAG systems can shift substantial work to
offline graph construction, indexing, or summary generation. SAGE
likewise has non-negligible offline graph-construction cost, but once
graph artifacts are cached, its core reader uses one primary graph read
plus at most a small number of pseudo-query reads, followed by sparse
entity-to-document projection.

The practical implication for self-evolving memory is not that the
entire SAGE agent has millisecond end-to-end latency. Rather, the
*graph-reader feedback path* is a reusable local computation once query
preparation and indices are available. This makes it possible to score
candidate memory graphs repeatedly without launching a new multi-round
agentic search for every graph candidate. At the same time, the measured
audit shows that a fully uncached online system is dominated by LM query
planning, NER/entity preparation, and answer generation, so the
retriever-only times in
Table [4](#S5.T4 "Table 4 ‣ Domain-specific Memory ‣ 5.1 End-to-End Effectiveness ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ SAGE: A Self-Evolving Agentic Graph-Memory Engine for Structure-AwareAssociative Memory")
must be interpreted within that narrower scope.

Finally, the paired long-term-memory host experiments in
Appendix [S](#A19 "Appendix S Integrate SAGE into Multiple Memory Systems ‣ SAGE: A Self-Evolving Agentic Graph-Memory Engine for Structure-AwareAssociative Memory")
are accuracy-oriented interventions. The available logs do not contain a
matched host-level end-to-end latency audit for every Native/$`+`$SAGE
configuration. We therefore do not infer host-level latency improvements
from the multi-hop reader timings reported here.

## Appendix K Implementation Details of Structured Query Planning

### K.1 Detailed definitions of the notation and additional information

In
$`\mathcal{P}_{\omega}(q)=\Big(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{exp}},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rel}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{hard}},\tau,\{(\tilde{q}_{m},\alpha_{m},t_{m})\}_{m=1}^{M}\Big)`$,
$`\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{exp}}`$ acts as a direct anchor for memory
(explicit entities); $`\mathcal{A}`$ maps the brain’s multiple
representational habits for the same concept (aliases);
$`\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rel}}`$ simulates the relational network in
semantic memory; $`\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{hard}}`$ serves as the
spatiotemporal and logical boundaries of episodic memory (such as hard
constraints like time and location); $`\tau`$ presets the cognitive
template of the target memory (answer type); and the pseudo-queries
$`\tilde{q}_{m}`$ with confidence $`\alpha_{m}`$ and intent $`t_{m}`$
are analogous to the multiple exploratory recalls conducted in the human
mind (Simulated Recall).

### K.2 Two-stage Planning: Extraction and Inference

Natural-language questions often compress key retrieval cues into
implicit relations, such as “the birthplace of the author”, “the
publication year of the only mystery novel of a certain work”, or “the
death date of the father”. If the original question is sent as a whole
to the entity linker, the system can easily hit only surface entities
while missing bridge entities or answer-type constraints. Therefore, the
query planner is defined as a structured function

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{P}(q)=\left(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{exp}},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rel}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{hard}},\tau,\{(\tilde{q}_{m},\alpha_{m})\}_{m=1}^{M}\right).
``` |  | (219) |

It consists of two stages:
*Extractor* [K.2](#A11.SS2 "K.2 Two-stage Planning: Extraction and Inference ‣ Appendix K Implementation Details of Structured Query Planning ‣ SAGE: A Self-Evolving Agentic Graph-Memory Engine for Structure-AwareAssociative Memory")
extracts explicit entities, aliases, relation clues, hard constraints,
and the answer type;
*Inferer* [K.2](#A11.SS2 "K.2 Two-stage Planning: Extraction and Inference ‣ Appendix K Implementation Details of Structured Query Planning ‣ SAGE: A Self-Evolving Agentic Graph-Memory Engine for Structure-AwareAssociative Memory")
generates at most $`M`$ retrieval intents based on the extraction
results.

## Appendix L Computation Details of Topological Structural Features

### L.1 Normalized Structural Graph

Structural features are computed on an undirected, self-loop-free,
binarized adjacency matrix $`\mathcal{A}_{s}`$:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{A}_{s}=\mathbb{I}[(\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{A}^{\top})>0],\qquad\operatorname{diag}(\mathcal{A}_{s})=0.
``` |  | (220) |

This avoids drastic fluctuations in topological statistics caused by
unstable relation-extraction directions. Message propagation can still
use the original bidirectional edges or relation-aware graph; structural
statistics are only used as gating conditions.

### L.2 Node-level Structural Features

For node $`v`$, let $`\mathcal{N}(v)=\{u:\mathcal{A}_{s,uv}=1\}`$ and
$`d_{v}=|\mathcal{N}(v)|`$. The node-level features are

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\phi(v)=\big[\log(1+d_{v}),c_{v},\kappa_{v},\bar{d}_{\mathcal{N}(v)}\big].
``` |  | (221) |

The local clustering coefficient is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
c_{v}=\begin{cases}\dfrac{2T_{v}}{d_{v}(d_{v}-1)},&d_{v}\geq 2,\\[3.99994pt]
0,&d_{v}<2,\end{cases}
``` |  | (222) |

where $`T_{v}`$ is the number of undirected edges inside the
neighborhood of $`v`$; $`\kappa_{v}`$ is the core number; and the
average neighbor degree is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\bar{d}_{\mathcal{N}(v)}=\begin{cases}\dfrac{1}{d_{v}}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}(v)}d_{u},&d_{v}>0,\\[3.99994pt]
0,&d_{v}=0.\end{cases}
``` |  | (223) |

These quantities respectively characterize node frequency, local
clustering, core/peripheral position, and neighborhood density. For RAG
memory, they correspond to four common structural risks:
over-propagation by high-frequency hubs, redundant diffusion inside
clustered regions, ignored peripheral bridge entities, and scale
mismatch between sparse and dense regions.

### L.3 Edge-pair Structural Features

For an undirected structural edge $`(u,v)`$, the pairwise features are

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\psi(u,v)=\big[|d_{u}-d_{v}|,\operatorname{CN}(u,v),\operatorname{Jac}(u,v)\big],
``` |  | (224) |

where

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\operatorname{CN}(u,v)=|\mathcal{N}(u)\cap\mathcal{N}(v)|,\qquad\operatorname{Jac}(u,v)=\frac{|\mathcal{N}(u)\cap\mathcal{N}(v)|}{|\mathcal{N}(u)\cup\mathcal{N}(v)|+\epsilon}.
``` |  | (225) |

Degree difference reflects cross-level connections, while common
neighbors and Jaccard reflect local community overlap. Based on these
features, the gate can distinguish intra-community evidence aggregation
edges from cross-community bridge edges.

### L.4 Graph-level Summary and Normalization

The graph-level summary concatenates the mean, standard deviation, and
density of node features:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbf{r}_{\mathcal{G}}=\big[\operatorname{mean}_{v\in\mathcal{V}}\phi(v);\operatorname{std}_{v\in\mathcal{V}}\phi(v);\operatorname{dens}(\mathcal{G})\big],
``` |  | (226) |

where

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\operatorname{dens}(\mathcal{G})=\begin{cases}\dfrac{2m_{s}}{n(n-1)},&n\geq 2,\\[3.99994pt]
0,&n<2,\end{cases}
``` |  | (227) |

$`n=|\mathcal{V}|`$, and $`m_{s}`$ is the number of undirected
structural edges. To remove graph-size differences, node and edge
features are z-scored within each graph, and the graph-level summary
computes global mean and standard deviation over the set of training
graphs:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\bar{\mathbf{r}}_{\mathcal{G}}=\frac{\mathbf{r}_{\mathcal{G}}-\mu_{r}}{\sigma_{r}+\epsilon}.
``` |  | (228) |

If the standard deviation of a certain dimension is close to zero, we
only perform centering to avoid division by an unstable small value.

### L.5 Gating Input Encoding

For each message edge $`u\to v`$, the structural gate reads the source
node, target node, pairwise features, and graph-level summary:

|  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\bar{\phi}(u)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\operatorname{NormNode}(\phi(u)),`$ | $`\displaystyle\bar{\psi}(u,v)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\operatorname{NormPair}(\psi(u,v)),`$ |  | (229) |
|  | $`\displaystyle u_{u}^{(l)}`$ | $`\displaystyle=E_{n}^{(l)}(\bar{\phi}(u)),`$ | $`\displaystyle u_{v}^{(l)}`$ | $`\displaystyle=E_{n}^{(l)}(\bar{\phi}(v)),`$ |  | (230) |
|  | $`\displaystyle v_{uv}^{(l)}`$ | $`\displaystyle=E_{p}^{(l)}(\bar{\psi}(u,v)),`$ | $`\displaystyle r_{\mathcal{G}}^{(l)}`$ | $`\displaystyle=E_{g}^{(l)}(\bar{\mathbf{r}}_{\mathcal{G}}).`$ |  | (231) |

The encoders $`E_{n},E_{p},E_{g}`$ are all two-layer MLPs. The
concatenated gating input is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbf{z}_{uv}^{(l)}=[\mathbf{u}_{u}^{(l)};\mathbf{u}_{v}^{(l)};\mathbf{v}_{uv}^{(l)};\mathbf{r}_{\mathcal{G}}^{(l)}].
``` |  | (232) |

The gate itself is a vector rather than a scalar:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbf{g}_{uv}^{(l)}=\mathbf{1}+\delta\tanh\big(\operatorname{MLP}_{g}^{(l)}(\mathbf{z}_{uv}^{(l)})\big),\qquad\delta=0.1.
``` |  | (233) |

The last layer of the gating MLP is initialized to zero, so initially
$`\mathbf{g}_{uv}^{(l)}=\mathbf{1}`$. At the beginning of training, the
model does not destroy the original propagation scale; the learned
structural bias gradually emerges in a residual manner.

### L.6 Message Propagation with Normalized Weights

Let $`\tilde{\mathbb{E}}`$ be the edge set after adding self-loops.
Structural gates are used for non-self-loop edges, and unit gates are
used for self-loops. The GCN normalization coefficient is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\eta_{uv}=\frac{w_{uv}}{\sqrt{\tilde{d}_{u}\tilde{d}_{v}}},\qquad\tilde{d}_{v}=\sum_{u:(u,v)\in\tilde{\mathbb{E}}}w_{uv},
``` |  | (234) |

where $`w_{uv}`$ defaults to $`1`$, but can also come from edge weights.
The propagation at layer $`l`$ is

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbf{m}_{u\to v}^{(l)}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\eta_{uv}\,\mathbf{g}_{uv}^{(l)}\odot W^{(l)}\mathbf{h}_{u}^{(l-1)},`$ |  | (235) |
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbf{h}_{v}^{(l)}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\sigma\left(\mathbf{b}^{(l)}+\sum_{u:(u,v)\in\tilde{\mathbb{E}}}\mathbf{m}_{u\to v}^{(l)}\right).`$ |  | (236) |

The multi-layer wrapper also contains inter-layer residuals: when
$`l>1`$ and the dimensions are consistent,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbf{H}^{(l)}\leftarrow\mathbf{H}^{(l)}+\mathbf{H}^{(l-1)}.
``` |  | (237) |

This residual and
Eq. ([233](#A12.E233 "Equation 233 ‣ L.5 Gating Input Encoding ‣ Appendix L Computation Details of Topological Structural Features ‣ SAGE: A Self-Evolving Agentic Graph-Memory Engine for Structure-AwareAssociative Memory"))
form a dual stability mechanism: the former stabilizes deep propagation,
while the latter stabilizes structural modulation.

### L.7 Chunked Gating and GPU Memory Complexity

Explicitly storing all gates requires $`O(|\mathbb{E}|d)`$ GPU memory.
For large graphs, gates are computed by edge chunks:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}=\bigcup_{b=1}^{B_{e}}\mathbb{E}_{b},\qquad|\mathbb{E}_{b}|\leq C_{e}.
``` |  | (238) |

Each edge chunk sequentially executes

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbf{g}_{b}\rightarrow\mathbf{m}_{b}\rightarrow\operatorname{scatter\_add}(\mathbf{m}_{b}),
``` |  | (239) |

and immediately releases the intermediate gate tensor. Online GPU memory
is reduced from $`O(|\mathbb{E}|d)`$ to $`O(C_{e}d)`$, while the time
complexity remains linear, $`O(|\mathbb{E}|d)`$. This is especially
important for self-evolving memory, because the same reader needs to
repeatedly evaluate candidate graphs produced by different writers.

## Appendix M Pretraining Objective and Augmented Views

### M.1 GraphCL View Construction

The goal of the pretraining stage is to learn cross-graph transferable
structural–semantic propagation, rather than fitting specific
question-answering labels. Given the original graph view
$`(\mathcal{G}_{0},X_{0})`$, we construct two augmented views
$`(\mathcal{G}_{1},X_{1})`$, $`(\mathcal{G}_{2},X_{2})`$ and one
negative feature view $`(\mathcal{G}_{0},X^{-})`$. The augmentation
types include edge perturbation, feature masking, node perturbation, and
subgraph sampling; let the augmentation operators be
$`\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}`$, then

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
(\mathcal{G}_{j},X_{j})=\mathcal{A}_{j}(\mathcal{G}_{0},X_{0}),\qquad j\in\{1,2\}.
``` |  | (240) |

If structural gating is enabled, each view precomputes its own node
structural features, edge-pair features, and graph-level summary; the
negative feature view shares the base graph structure, but its node
features are shuffled or replaced.

### M.2 Graph-level Contrastive Objective

The encoder outputs four sets of node representations:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
H_{0}=f_{\theta}(X_{0},\mathcal{G}_{0}),\quad H_{1}=f_{\theta}(X_{1},\mathcal{G}_{1}),\quad H_{2}=f_{\theta}(X_{2},\mathcal{G}_{2}),\quad H^{-}=f_{\theta}(X^{-},\mathcal{G}_{0}).
``` |  | (241) |

The graph readout of each augmented view is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
c_{j}=\operatorname{sigmoid}\left(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{V}_{j}|}\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{j}}H_{j,v}\right),\qquad j\in\{1,2\}.
``` |  | (242) |

The bilinear discriminator

|     |                       |     |       |
|-----|-----------------------|-----|-------|
|     |
       ``` math
       D(c,h)=h^{\top}W_{D}c
       ```                    |     | (243) |

determines whether the node representation comes from the same graph
semantics. The pretraining loss is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{GCL}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\left[\operatorname{BCE}\big(D(c_{j},H_{0}),\mathbf{1}\big)+\operatorname{BCE}\big(D(c_{j},H^{-}),\mathbf{0}\big)\right].
``` |  | (244) |

When edge-level gating is enabled, traditional static structural prompts
are neutralized into identity mappings to avoid scale confusion caused
by two sets of structural modulations acting simultaneously; the
structural bias is mainly carried by the target edge’s
$`\mathbf{g}_{uv}^{(l)}`$.

### M.3 Feature Alignment Layer

When the input dimensions produced by different graphs or different text
encoders are consistent but their distributions have large shifts, the
feature alignment layer can be enabled:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\operatorname{Align}(x)=\operatorname{Dropout}\left(\operatorname{LayerNorm}\left(\operatorname{PReLU}(W_{a}x+b_{a})\right)\right).
``` |  | (245) |

$`W_{a}`$ is initialized as the identity matrix, and $`b_{a}`$ is
initialized as zero. Therefore, this layer is initially an approximately
identity transformation; after training, it absorbs inter-graph
feature-scale differences without changing the core structure of the
graph propagator.

## Appendix N Supervised Fine-tuning Objective

### N.1 Entity-level Supervision

For each question $`q_{b}`$, the data provide a supporting-entity mask
$`y_{b,e}\in\{0,1\}`$. The model outputs entity logits $`a_{b,e}`$. The
weighted BCE is defined as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{bce}}=\frac{1}{B}\sum_{b=1}^{B}\frac{\sum_{e}w_{b,e}\,\operatorname{BCEWithLogits}(a_{b,e},y_{b,e})}{\sum_{e}w_{b,e}+\epsilon}.
``` |  | (246) |

Positive weights are uniformly normalized within the positive set; if
the adversarial temperature $`T_{a}`$ is enabled for negative weights,
they are computed by applying softmax to the current model scores:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
w_{b,e}^{-}=\frac{\exp(a_{b,e}/T_{a})}{\sum_{v:y_{b,v}=0}\exp(a_{b,v}/T_{a})},\qquad y_{b,e}=0.
``` |  | (247) |

If $`T_{a}=0`$, the negative weights degenerate into a uniform
distribution. This design makes training focus more on high-scoring hard
negatives, rather than being dominated by a large number of obviously
irrelevant entities.

### N.2 Multi-positive List Cross-Entropy

Using only BCE treats each entity as an independent binary
classification problem, lacking the constraint that “supporting entities
should collectively rank near the top of the same candidate list”. To
this end, we introduce a multi-positive list loss. Let

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
p_{b,e}=\frac{\operatorname{sigmoid}(a_{b,e})}{\sum_{v}\operatorname{sigmoid}(a_{b,v})+\epsilon}.
``` |  | (248) |

If sample $`b`$ has at least one supporting entity, the list loss is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{list}}=-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{B}_{+}|}\sum_{b\in\mathcal{B}_{+}}\frac{1}{|Y_{E}(q_{b})|}\sum_{e\in Y_{E}(q_{b})}\log(p_{b,e}+\epsilon).
``` |  | (249) |

Samples with empty supporting-entity sets are skipped. The final entity
fine-tuning objective is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{ent}}=\lambda_{\mathrm{bce}}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{bce}}+\lambda_{\mathrm{list}}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{list}},\qquad(\lambda_{\mathrm{bce}},\lambda_{\mathrm{list}})=(0.3,0.7).
``` |  | (250) |

### N.3 Optional Document-level Supervision

If the training configuration provides a document-level loss, entity
logits are first projected into document logits:

|     |                                       |     |       |
|-----|---------------------------------------|-----|-------|
|     |
       ``` math
       \tilde{S}_{b}=a_{b}^{\top}\mathbf{M},
       ```                                    |     | (251) |

and then the same type of BCE or list loss is computed with the
supporting-document mask $`z_{b,i}`$. This term is suitable for tasks
where entity annotations are noisy but the document support set is
reliable; if it is not enabled, training is entirely driven by the
entity-level support set, and document ranking is obtained through
projection only during inference or validation.

## Appendix O Memory Writer Implementation Details

### O.1 The Markov Decision Process for Multi-turn Graph Construction

Specifically, the training of our graph constructor is implemented
through VeRL’s multi-turn GRPO loop. The state machine of the interactor
can be abstracted as a finite-horizon MDP:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},P,\mathcal{R},\rho_{0},H).
``` |  | (252) |

Given a sample $`x`$, at round $`t`$, the state can be written as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
s_{t}=(q,\mathcal{G}_{t},\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{proc}}_{t},\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{rem}}_{t},\zeta_{t}),
``` |  | (253) |

where $`\mathcal{G}_{t}`$ is the current partially written graph,
$`\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{proc}}_{t}`$ and
$`\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{rem}}_{t}`$ denote the processed and remaining
documents, respectively, and $`\zeta_{t}`$ is an interaction control
flag, such as whether the process is still in the graph-construction
stage or has already switched to the RAG stage. The action is generated
by the language model in JSON format:

|     |                                         |     |       |
|-----|-----------------------------------------|-----|-------|
|     |
       ``` math
       a_{t}\sim\pi_{\theta}(\cdot\mid s_{t}),
       ```                                      |     | (254) |

and is restricted to two types of legal actions:

1.  1.
    Triple action: output a JSON array, where each element is of the
    form $`\{\texttt{subject},\texttt{relation},\texttt{object}\}`$,
    representing the set of facts $`\mathcal{T}_{t}`$ written in the
    current round;
2.  2.
    Termination action: after graph construction is completed, output a
    JSON object carrying the terminal fields required by the reader
    side, such as answer, recall, precision, deducible, and so on.

In implementation, the environment first checks whether the action can
be parsed by json_repair, and strictly cleans the triples: items with
missing keys, empty strings, or non-dictionary entries are all removed.
If illegal JSON is output during the graph-construction stage, the
interaction terminates immediately and returns zero reward; if legal
triples are output, the environment proceeds to the next round and
returns a round-level format reward. The corresponding environment
transition can be written as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
s_{t+1}=P(s_{t},a_{t})=\begin{cases}(q,\mathcal{G}_{t}\oplus\mathcal{T}_{t},\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{proc}}_{t}\cup\{d_{t}\},\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{rem}}_{t}\setminus\{d_{t}\},\zeta_{t+1}),&a_{t}\text{ is legal },\\
(q,\mathcal{G}_{t},\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{proc}}_{t},\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{rem}}_{t},\mathrm{STOP}),&a_{t}\text{ is illegal},\\
(q,\mathcal{G}_{t},\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{proc}}_{t},\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{rem}}_{t},\mathrm{RAG}),&a_{t}\text{ triggers the reading stage}.\end{cases}
``` |  | (255) |

##### Iterative and non-iterative writing.

Two strategies are supported. In the non-iterative mode, the model reads
the entire context $`\mathcal{D}`$ at once and outputs all triples. In
the iterative mode, the environment reads the documents segment by
segment in document order, and in each round the model is only allowed
to write triples for the current document. After all documents have been
processed, the environment then switches to the RAG stage. If
$`\mathcal{T}_{i}`$ denotes the set of triples output for document
$`d_{i}`$, then the final graph constructed in the iterative mode is
$`\mathcal{G}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{T}_{i}`$, where $`\oplus`$
denotes edge-set union and node deduplication. We adopt the iterative
strategy by default, because it decomposes the long-context problem into
a sequence of local writing decisions, significantly reducing the
difficulty of performing global planning in advance. At the same time,
it also allows the source document of each triple to be precisely
recorded, providing explicit source edges for subsequent text-graph
retrieval.

##### Constructing text-graph memory from output triples.

To enable the frozen retriever to operate under the graph-guided text
retrieval setting, the environment does not directly pass the raw triple
strings to the retriever. Instead, it first constructs a text graph with
document nodes:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{V}_{e}\cup\mathcal{V}_{d},\mathcal{E}_{ee}\cup\mathcal{E}_{ed}),
``` |  | (256) |

where the entity node set $`\mathcal{V}_{e}`$ comes from the subjects
and objects in the triples, and the document node set
$`\mathcal{V}_{d}=\{d_{1},\dots,d_{m}\}`$ corresponds to the original
documents in the context. The entity-entity edges are defined as

|     |                                                        |     |       |
|-----|--------------------------------------------------------|-----|-------|
|     |
       ``` math
       \mathcal{E}_{ee}=\{(u,r,v)\mid(u,r,v)\in\mathcal{T}\},
       ```                                                     |     | (257) |

and the entity-document source edges are defined as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{E}_{ed}=\{(u,\texttt{source},d_{i}),(v,\texttt{source},d_{i})\mid(u,r,v)\in\mathcal{T}_{i}\}.
``` |  | (258) |

In the iterative mode, the source edges are explicit, because the
environment already knows that the triples in each round come from the
current document. In the non-iterative mode, we use a heuristic
alignment method based on tokenizer token overlap to map each triple to
the most similar document. The significance of this design is that,
after separating writing from reading, the graph constructor is only
responsible for deciding “what to write into memory”; as for how the
reader aggregates entities on the graph and retrieves documents, this is
entirely determined by the frozen $`f_{\phi}`$.

##### Frozen GFM retrieval environment.

When training the graph constructor, the reader $`f_{\phi}`$ is fixed as
the already trained GFM retriever. Let the entity set be
$`\mathcal{V}_{e}=\{e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\}`$ and the document set be
$`\mathcal{V}_{d}=\{d_{1},\dots,d_{M}\}`$. We then construct:

1.  1.
    the relation-edge index $`\mathbf{E}`$ with both forward and reverse
    directions, together with the relation types $`\mathbf{r}`$;
2.  2.
    the sparse entity-document matrix
    $`\mathbf{M}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times M}`$, where $`M_{ij}=1`$ if entity
    $`e_{i}`$ appears in document $`d_{j}`$;
3.  3.
    the question-related entity mask $`\mathbf{m}_{q}\in\{0,1\}^{n}`$,
    which is obtained preferentially through lexical matching with the
    question; if lexical matching fails, it falls back to a heuristic
    seed set ranked by entity degree.

After encoding the question as a vector $`\mathbf{q}`$ and the relation
names as a matrix $`\mathbf{R}`$, the frozen GFM forward pass computes
the entity relevance scores:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbf{s}_{e}=f_{\phi}(\mathcal{G},\mathbf{q},\mathbf{m}_{q};\phi)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.
``` |  | (259) |

The entity scores are then projected into document scores. Let
$`\mathbf{M}_{\mathrm{Top}\text{-}k}(\mathbf{s}_{e})`$ denote the
masking operation that retains only the top-$`K`$ entity scores, and let
$`\mathbf{w}_{\mathrm{idf}}`$ denote the inverse-frequency weights
defined according to the document frequency of each entity. The four
document-scoring modes can be written uniformly as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{e}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{s}_{e},&\texttt{raw},\\
\mathbf{M}_{\mathrm{Top}\text{-}k}(\mathbf{s}_{e}),&\texttt{topk},\\
\mathbf{w}_{\mathrm{idf}}\odot\mathbf{s}_{e},&\texttt{idf},\\
\mathbf{w}_{\mathrm{idf}}\odot\mathbf{M}_{\mathrm{Top}\text{-}k}(\mathbf{s}_{e}),&\texttt{idf\_topk},\end{cases}
``` |  | (260) |

and the document scores are obtained by

|     |                                                         |     |       |
|-----|---------------------------------------------------------|-----|-------|
|     |
       ``` math
       \mathbf{s}_{d}=\mathbf{M}^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{e}.
       ```                                                      |     | (261) |

We then take $`\mathrm{Top}\text{-}k(\mathbf{s}_{d})`$ as the retrieval
result. In actual use, we also enable init_entities_weight, that is,
during the GFM forward pass, a $`1/f(e)`$ weight is applied to
high-frequency entities to suppress the dominance of entities connected
to too many documents in the retrieval results.

### P.1 Path Interpretations

We provide path interpretations of SAGE for multi-hop reasoning in
[Table 18](#A16.T18 "In P.1 Path Interpretations ‣ Appendix P Additional Detailed Experimental Results ‣ SAGE: A Self-Evolving Agentic Graph-Memory Engine for Structure-AwareAssociative Memory").
The importance of each path to the final prediction can be measured by
the partial derivative of the prediction score with respect to the
triples at each reasoning layer. The top-$`k`$ path interpretations are
then obtained by selecting the top-$`k`$ longest paths with beam search.

As shown in
[Table 18](#A16.T18 "In P.1 Path Interpretations ‣ Appendix P Additional Detailed Experimental Results ‣ SAGE: A Self-Evolving Agentic Graph-Memory Engine for Structure-AwareAssociative Memory"),
SAGE successfully identifies the answer by connecting two key
constraints in the question: the person who presented the Australia 2022
FIFA World Cup bid and the person born on October 22, 1930.
Specifically, the first path starts from the entity “the bid for the
2022 FIFA World Cup” and follows the inverse relation of “was one of the
representatives of” to reach “Frank Lowy”. Then, through an
entity-equivalence relation, it links “Frank Lowy” to “Sir Frank P.
Lowy”, whose birth date is “22 October 1930”. The second path verifies
the reasoning in the reverse direction by starting from the birth date
and tracing back to the representative of the World Cup bid. These paths
demonstrate that SAGE can effectively align different surface forms of
the same entity and integrate multiple question constraints within a
single-step retrieval process, showing its ability to perform
interpretable multi-hop reasoning.

[TABLE]

Table 18: Path interpretations for multi-hop reasoning, where $`r^{-1}`$
denotes the inverse of original relation.
````
