````math
## Appendix Q Dataset Details

Table [19](#A17.T19 "Table 19 ‣ Appendix Q Dataset Details ‣ SAGE: A Self-Evolving Agentic Graph-Memory Engine for Structure-AwareAssociative Memory")
summarizes the details of each dataset.

[TABLE]

Table 19: Dataset statistics and evaluation scenarios. We evaluate SAGE
on three complementary categories: general and multi-hop QA, practical
e-commerce review QA, and long-term agent memory. “Train/Dev/Test”
denotes the standard split when available. For benchmark-only datasets
without a conventional supervised training split, we report the total
number of evaluation instances or benchmark scale.

##### General and Multi-hop QA.

We first evaluate SAGE on a set of general open-domain and multi-hop QA
benchmarks that stress different aspects of retrieval-augmented
reasoning. NQ-Open is derived from Natural Questions and is widely used
as a standard open-domain short-answer QA benchmark; it evaluates
whether a system can retrieve and ground factual answers from a large
Wikipedia-scale corpus. PopQA complements NQ by focusing on
entity-centric factual questions whose subjects span different
popularity levels, making it particularly useful for testing whether a
retrieval or memory system can recover long-tail factual knowledge
rather than relying only on parametric memorization. HotpotQA contains
Wikipedia-based multi-hop questions with sentence-level supporting
facts, allowing us to evaluate not only answer correctness but also
whether the system can recover bridge evidence and produce interpretable
reasoning chains. 2WikiMultiHopQA further stresses structured multi-hop
reasoning by combining Wikipedia text with Wikidata-derived relations
and providing evidence paths for 2–4 hop questions. Finally, MuSiQue is
designed to reduce shortcut reasoning by composing connected single-hop
questions into 2–4 hop questions, making it a strong testbed for
evaluating whether SAGE can retrieve and integrate multiple pieces of
evidence in a genuinely compositional manner.

##### E-commerce Review-based QA.

We use AmazonQA to evaluate SAGE in a practical, noisy, user-generated
e-commerce setting. Unlike Wikipedia-style QA benchmarks, AmazonQA
consists of real product questions, community answers, product reviews,
and product metadata, and includes answerability annotations indicating
whether a question can be answered from available reviews. This makes it
a suitable benchmark for testing whether a memory system can identify
useful evidence from noisy review collections, distinguish answerable
from unanswerable questions, and synthesize grounded answers from
multiple user-generated snippets. From the perspective of self-evolving
memory, AmazonQA is especially valuable because the system must learn
which review facts, product attributes, and user opinions are worth
indexing for future retrieval, rather than simply matching a question to
a clean encyclopedic passage.

##### Long-term Agent Memory.

To move beyond conventional RAG evaluation, we further evaluate SAGE on
long-term agent memory benchmarks. LongMemEval is designed to assess the
long-term memory abilities of chat assistants over extended
multi-session interaction histories. It covers five core memory
abilities: information extraction, multi-session reasoning, temporal
reasoning, knowledge updates, and abstention. This benchmark directly
tests whether SAGE can retrieve sparse but relevant memory traces from
long histories, combine evidence across sessions, respect temporal
order, and update previously stored information when new interactions
supersede old memories. We use HaluMem as a complementary benchmark for
evaluating hallucination in memory systems. Rather than only measuring
end-to-end QA accuracy, HaluMem decomposes memory evaluation into memory
extraction, memory updating, and memory question answering, thereby
revealing at which operational stage hallucinations, omissions, or
conflicts arise. This is particularly important for our setting because
errors introduced during graph construction or memory updating may
propagate to graph-guided retrieval and final answer generation.

##### Evaluation Rationale.

Together, these datasets form a progressively broader evaluation suite.
NQ and PopQA test factual open-domain retrieval; HotpotQA,
2WikiMultiHopQA, and MuSiQue test multi-hop evidence composition;
AmazonQA evaluates noisy real-world review memory in an e-commerce
domain; LongMemEval tests long-horizon interactive memory; and HaluMem
diagnoses operation-level hallucinations in memory systems. This
combination allows us to evaluate SAGE not merely as a
retrieval-augmented QA pipeline, but as a self-evolving memory system
that must decide what to store, how to organize stored information, how
to retrieve it under different query conditions, and how to update or
suppress unreliable memories over time.

## Appendix R Baselines and Metrics

##### Baselines.

We evaluate SAGE against state-of-the-art baselines, including their
combined variants, which are grouped into four categories:

- •
  Base LLM: GPT-4o-mini [Hurst et al. (2024)](#bib.bib11).
- •
  Single-step RAGs: including BM25 [Robertson and Walker
  (1994)](#bib.bib12), Contriever [Izacard et al. (2021)](#bib.bib13),
  GTR [Ni et al. (2022)](#bib.bib14), ColBERTv2 [Santhanam et al.
  (2022)](#bib.bib15), RAPTOR [Sarthi et al. (2024)](#bib.bib16), and
  Proposition [Chen et al. (2024)](#bib.bib17).
- •
  Graph-enhanced RAGs: including GraphRAG [Edge et al.
  (2024)](#bib.bib18), G-Retriever [He et al. (2024)](#bib.bib19),
  LightRAG [Guo et al. (2024)](#bib.bib20), HippoRAG [Gutiérrez et al.
  (2024)](#bib.bib27), HippoRAG 2 [Gutiérrez et al. (2025)](#bib.bib21),
  SubgraphRAG [Li et al. (2024)](#bib.bib6), PropRAG [Wang and Han
  (2025)](#bib.bib22), and the closely related GFM-RAG [Luo et al.
  (2025)](#bib.bib5).
- •
  Multi-step RAGs: IRCoT [Trivedi et al. (2023)](#bib.bib25),
  FLARE [Jiang et al. (2023)](#bib.bib24), and Adaptive-RAG [Jeong et
  al. (2024)](#bib.bib23).

In particular, IRCoT [Trivedi et al. (2023)](#bib.bib25) is a general
multi-step reasoning framework that can be integrated with non-iterative
retrievers, allowing both single-step RAG and graph-based methods to
conduct multi-hop reasoning through interleaved retrieval and
generation.

##### Metrics.

To evaluate retrieval quality, we report Recall@2 and Recall@5 for both
retrieved entities and documents, denoted as R@2/5$`{}_{\textsf{E}}`$
and R@2/5$`{}_{\textsf{D}}`$, respectively. For end-to-end QA
evaluation, we use standard metrics, including Exact Match (EM), F1
score, Precision (P), and Recall (R), in the main experiments to
comprehensively measure answer correctness and coverage.

## Appendix S Integrate SAGE into Multiple Memory Systems

### S.1 Host implementations

##### Zep.

The native context budget is 16,384 tokens. Extraction uses GPT-4o-mini;
answer generation and judging use GPT-4o with temperature zero.

##### Memobase.

We use Memobase v0.0.42. Both arms share ingestion, durable memory
state, profiles, queries, reference times, local Qwen3-Embedding-8B
embeddings, and answer configuration. Extraction and summarization use
GPT-4o-mini; answers and judges request GPT-4o at temperature zero.

##### MemOS.

Native embeddings and the SAGE branch use all-mpnet-base-v2. The graph
index is prebuilt: normal MemOS writes do not synchronize new memories
into that index online. LongMemEval answers and judges use GPT-4o-mini;
HaluMem answers and judges use GPT-4o. The MemReader extraction
temperature is 0.6, distinct from the zero-temperature answer/judge
settings.

### S.2 Deployment cost

|                      |                      |         |          |
|----------------------|----------------------|---------|----------|
| Host/task            | Stage                | Native  | SAGE     |
| Memobase HaluMem     | Retrieval mean       | 253.1   | 297.9    |
|                      | Retrieval p95        | 393.5   | 523.2    |
| Memobase LongMemEval | Retrieval mean       | 221.8   | 314.5    |
|                      | Retrieval p95        | 287.1   | 376.0    |
|                      | Retrieve+answer mean | 2,192.3 | 2,795.7  |
| MemOS LongMemEval    | Search mean          | –       | 70,385.9 |
|                      | Search+answer mean   | –       | 72,110.8 |

Table 20: Descriptive latency measurements. All entries are
milliseconds. Retrieve-plus-answer excludes the offline evaluator.

## Appendix T Matched Retrieval Protocol for Restricted-Corpus Transfer

### T.1 Evaluation unit and restricted corpora

The NQ and PopQA measurements use 1,000 queries each and restricted
document corpora. NQ contains 964 documents and 17,035 passages after
256-token segmentation; PopQA contains 1,793 documents and 8,722
passages.

Each retriever produces ten documents. The retrieved documents are
segmented into passages and reranked using the same MPNet passage
reranker. Five passages are supplied to the common answer generator. All
1,000 queries in each dataset were evaluated without dropping failed or
incomplete cases in the reported matched rerun.

### T.2 Retrieval metric

Passage R@k is a question-level hit rate: a query succeeds if at least
one selected passage contains a normalized gold-answer string. Gold
answers are used only for scoring, not for retrieval or reranking. This
metric differs from fractional recall of annotated support documents.
Retrieving a support page does not ensure that an answer-bearing passage
appears in the final context. Conversely, a valid answer-bearing passage
may come from a document that is not one of the annotated support pages.

The current paper uses the passage-level metric consistently and does
not compare it numerically with an earlier document-title-based
measurement. QA EM/F1 is reported alongside retrieval hit rate to
measure the final answer effect under the same context-construction
protocol.

### T.3 Meaning of no-target-adaptation(0-shot) transfer

The writer and reader were trained on HotpotQA, 2WikiMultiHopQA, and
MuSiQue, with no NQ- or PopQA-specific gradient updates, tuning, or
evolution rounds for the reported transfer setting. This is
target-task/label transfer, not a claim of novel Wikipedia content or
complete absence of knowledge in generic pretrained encoders and LLMs.

## Appendix U Diagnostic Analysis of Memory Failures

This appendix analyzes *where* long-term-memory errors arise rather than
treating every incorrect answer as a retrieval failure. The audit is
based on the earlier standalone SAGE memory runs and is reported
separately from the paired host-integration experiments in
Appendix [S](#A19 "Appendix S Integrate SAGE into Multiple Memory Systems ‣ SAGE: A Self-Evolving Agentic Graph-Memory Engine for Structure-AwareAssociative Memory").
The audited sets contain 110 LongMemEval examples and 763 HaluMem-Medium
examples. Its purpose is diagnostic: it localizes missing evidence,
ranking failures, and post-retrieval reasoning failures, but it is not
used as the primary system-level comparison for the host-integration
results.

### U.1 Diagnostic Protocol and Scope

##### Sequential failure attribution.

For an incorrect question, we assign the first applicable stage in the
following order:

1.  1.
    Writer omission / source-anchor failure. At least one annotated gold
    memory is absent from the stored corpus, or a gold document has no
    usable entity in the document2entities mapping and therefore cannot
    be anchored into the KG.
2.  2.
    Graph reachability failure. The gold memories are written and
    anchorable, but at least one required gold document lies outside the
    two-hop *undirected* KG neighborhood of the exact-query seed
    entities.
3.  3.
    Top-$`k`$ ranking failure. All required gold documents are
    structurally reachable under the above proxy, but at least one
    required document is absent from the reader’s top-10 retrieved
    context.
4.  4.
    Answer-generation failure. All annotated gold documents are present
    in the top-10 context, but the final answer is still scored
    incorrect.

Because the stages are applied sequentially, they are mutually exclusive
within each audited question set.

##### What the diagnostic does *not* establish.

The two-hop neighborhood is a structural proxy, not a reconstruction of
the actual GFM computation path. A globally initialized or deeper reader
can in principle use evidence outside this proxy, and the presence of a
two-hop path does not prove that the GFM actually propagated information
along that path. Similarly, the source-anchor test detects a missing or
unusable anchor; it does not verify that an existing anchor is
semantically the correct entity, because the benchmarks do not provide
gold entity-level anchoring annotations. Finally, the last stage
localizes an error to the post-retrieval portion of the pipeline, but it
can include answer selection, temporal arithmetic, aggregation, polarity
conversion, or evaluator mismatch rather than a single unique causal
mechanism.

### U.2 Gold-Memory and Source-Anchor Coverage

Both audited datasets have 100% gold-memory coverage: the annotated
evidence is present in the stored corpus for every audited question. The
category-specific source-anchor audit is shown in
Table [21](#A21.T21 "Table 21 ‣ U.2 Gold-Memory and Source-Anchor Coverage ‣ Appendix U Diagnostic Analysis of Memory Failures ‣ SAGE: A Self-Evolving Agentic Graph-Memory Engine for Structure-AwareAssociative Memory").

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
| Dataset / type | Gold documents | Written in corpus | Usable KG anchor |
| LongMemEval KU | 36 | 36 | 36 |
| LongMemEval TR | 61 | 61 | 61 |
| LongMemEval MS | 82 | 82 | 82 |
| HaluMem KU | 62 | 62 | 62 |
| HaluMem temporal-cue proxy | 810 | 810 | 806 |
| HaluMem MS | 93 | 93 | 93 |

Table 21: Gold-document writing and source-anchor coverage in the
diagnostic audit. “Usable KG anchor” means that the gold document has at
least one entity in the document2entities mapping. HaluMem’s temporal
row is a cue-based proxy rather than an official benchmark category.

The four anchor-incomplete documents in the HaluMem temporal-cue proxy
occur only in negative/unknown questions for which the model correctly
returns an unknown answer. They therefore do not contribute a
writer/source-anchor failure among the incorrect temporal-proxy
examples. This result should not be read as proof that the writer never
creates a semantically wrong anchor; the proxy only tests whether an
anchor is available.

### U.3 LongMemEval: Overall and Category-Level Attribution

Across all 110 audited LongMemEval questions, 38 are correct, 27 are
assigned to graph-reachability failure, 27 to top-10 ranking failure,
and 18 to answer-generation failure. No question is assigned to writer
omission under the above gold-memory-coverage test. Thus, the errors are
distributed across structure, ranking, and post-retrieval reasoning
rather than being explained by one single stage.

| Outcome / stage      | Count | Share  |
| Correct              | 38    | 34.55% |
| Writer/source-anchor | 0     | 0.00%  |

| Top-10 ranking       | 27    | 24.55% |
| Answer generation    | 18    | 16.36% |

LongMemEval audit.

LongMemEval provides official question types including Knowledge Update

three subsets contain 18, 29, and 32 audited questions, respectively.
The category-level decomposition is reported in
Table [23](#A21.T23 "Table 23 ‣ U.3 LongMemEval: Overall and Category-Level Attribution ‣ Appendix U Diagnostic Analysis of Memory Failures ‣ SAGE: A Self-Evolving Agentic Graph-Memory Engine for Structure-AwareAssociative Memory").
Percentages in the last four columns use the *incorrect examples of that
category* as the denominator.

|      |       |         |        |               |             |             |             |
|------|-------|---------|--------|---------------|-------------|-------------|-------------|
| Type | Total | Correct | Errors | Writer/anchor | Graph       | Top-$`k`$   | Generation  |
| KU   | 18    | 5       | 13     | 0 (0.00%)     | 4 (30.77%)  | 7 (53.85%)  | 2 (15.38%)  |
| TR   | 29    | 7       | 22     | 0 (0.00%)     | 3 (13.64%)  | 9 (40.91%)  | 10 (45.45%) |

Table 23: LongMemEval category-level error attribution. Each entry in
the failure columns is count (percentage of the incorrect examples in
that row).

#### U.3.1 Knowledge Update: Missing the Latest State or Selecting a Stale One

For LongMemEval KU, 7/13 errors (53.85%) are top-10 ranking failures,
4/13 (30.77%) are structural-reachability failures, and 2/13 (15.38%)
remain after all annotated evidence has entered the answer context. The
dominant pattern is therefore not that the update was never written;
rather, the latest state is inaccessible, is ranked below an older
state, or is available but not treated as superseding the stale fact.

##### Case KU-1: latest fact written but structurally unreachable.

For longmemeval_oracle_train_159, the question is “How many issues of
National Geographic have I finished reading?” The gold answer is *five*,
whereas the model answers *three issues*. An earlier session saying that
three issues had been completed is ranked first. A later session
explicitly stating that the fifth issue has been finished is present in
memory, but it does not enter the top-10 and lies outside the two-hop
neighborhood of the exact-query seed. The failure chain is therefore

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\text{latest fact written}\rightarrow\text{latest session structurally unreachable}\rightarrow\text{old session retrieved}\rightarrow\text{stale answer}.
``` |  |

##### Case KU-2: latest fact reachable but ranked out.

For longmemeval_oracle_train_142, the question asks “How many MCU films
did I watch in the last 3 months?” The gold answer is 5 and the model
answers 4. The older four-film session is ranked eighth. The later
session updating the count to five is written and structurally
reachable, but it does not enter the top-10. This isolates a
reader-ranking failure from a memory-writing failure.

##### Case KU-3: both versions retrieved, but the answerer selects the obsolete value.

For longmemeval_oracle_train_137, the question asks “How many stars do I
need to reach the gold level on my Starbucks Rewards app?” The updated
value, 120 stars, is ranked first; the older 125-star memory is ranked
third. The model nevertheless answers 125. Because the newer fact is
both retrieved and ranked above the stale fact, this is a post-retrieval
update-resolution failure: the answerer does not use the update relation
to invalidate the obsolete value.

#### U.3.2 Temporal Reasoning: Evidence Omission versus Temporal Computation

LongMemEval TR exhibits a different profile. Among its 22 errors, 9
(40.91%) are ranking failures and 10 (45.45%) occur after all annotated
evidence is already in the top-10. A finer audit identifies 12 errors
with temporal-evidence omission, 7 with duration/time-difference
mistakes, and 3 with ordering or temporal-qualifier mistakes. Therefore,
improving retrieval alone cannot resolve the full TR error set.

##### Case TR-1: both temporal endpoints reachable but omitted from top-10.

For longmemeval_oracle_train_6, the question is “How many days did it
take for me to find a house I loved after starting to work with Rachel?”
The required sessions record February 15 as the start of working with
Rachel and March 1 as the date on which a liked house was found. Both
sessions are structurally reachable, but neither appears in the top-10,
so the answerer cannot reconstruct the interval and returns unknown
instead of the gold 14 days.

##### Case TR-2: both dates retrieved, but duration arithmetic is wrong.

For longmemeval_oracle_train_27, the question asks for the number of
days between buying the Adidas running shoes and the Converse shoelaces
breaking. The January 10 purchase is ranked first and the January 24
shoelace event is ranked tenth. Both temporal endpoints are therefore
available, but the model answers 25 days instead of the correct 14 days.
This is a post-retrieval duration-arithmetic failure.

##### Case TR-3: both dates retrieved, but event ordering is reversed.

For longmemeval_oracle_train_32, the question is “Which device did I set
up first, the smart thermostat or the new router?” The router event is
dated January 15 and ranked first; the thermostat event is dated
February 10 and ranked third. Despite retrieving both dates, the model
answers that the thermostat came first. The evidence acquisition
succeeds, but the answerer mishandles the temporal ordering implied by
*first*.

#### U.3.3 Multi-Session Reasoning: Incomplete Support Coverage and Aggregation

MS is the LongMemEval category most strongly associated with
graph/evidence coverage. Graph reachability and top-10 ranking jointly
account for 24/28 errors (85.72%). The main retrieval-side problem is
that evidence distributed across several sessions is not simultaneously
available; when all sessions are available, aggregation, category
boundaries, and arithmetic can still fail.

##### Case MS-1: only part of a distributed weekly pattern is retrieved.

For longmemeval_oracle_train_94, the question is “How many fitness
classes do I attend in a typical week?” The gold answer is 5. The
required evidence is distributed over several sessions, including Monday
BodyPump, Saturday Hip Hop Abs, Sunday yoga, and other weekly classes.
Only the Monday BodyPump session enters the top-10; one key document
lies outside the two-hop proxy and other required sessions are also
absent from the reader context. Because “a typical week” is not itself a
single explicit entity, the reader does not recover a stable bridge that
connects all of the distributed supporting sessions.

##### Case MS-2: all annotated evidence retrieved, but aggregation is incorrect.

For longmemeval_oracle_train_114, the answer requires combining 17 poems
(rank 7), 5 short stories (rank 5), and one writing-challenge piece
(rank 1). All supporting evidence is present, so the correct total is
$`17+5+1=23`$. The model answers 24 and incorrectly expands the number
of short stories. This case isolates multi-session aggregation, category
boundaries, and arithmetic from retrieval coverage.

### U.4 HaluMem: Category-Level Attribution

HaluMem-Medium shows a more concentrated retrieval bottleneck than
LongMemEval. Across the 763-example audit, gold-memory coverage is 100%.
Moreover, 98.30% of questions have all annotated gold memories inside
the two-hop structural proxy. In contrast, only 21.76% have at least one
gold memory in the reader top-10, and only 6.42% have *all* gold
memories in the top-10. Conditioned on structural reachability, the
resulting top-$`k`$ ranking-failure rate is 93.47%.

|                                               |         |
|-----------------------------------------------|---------|
| Metric                                        | Result  |
| Gold memory coverage                          | 100.00% |
| All gold two-hop reachable                    | 98.30%  |
| At least one gold in top-10                   | 21.76%  |
| All gold in top-10                            | 6.42%   |
| Top-$`k`$ failure conditioned on reachability | 93.47%  |

Table 24: HaluMem structural-coverage diagnostics.

HaluMem provides an official *Dynamic Update* category, which we denote
KU, and an official *Multi-hop Inference* category, which we denote MS.
It does *not* provide an official TR category. For diagnostic purposes
only, we construct a temporal-cue proxy consisting of questions
containing expressions such as *latest*, *current*, *as of*, *before*,
*after*, *by*, or *how long*. This proxy contains 387 examples and can
overlap both KU and MS; consequently, the rows of
Table [25](#A21.T25 "Table 25 ‣ U.4 HaluMem: Category-Level Attribution ‣ Appendix U Diagnostic Analysis of Memory Failures ‣ SAGE: A Self-Evolving Agentic Graph-Memory Engine for Structure-AwareAssociative Memory")
must not be summed.

|  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|
| Type | Total | Correct | Errors | Writer/anchor | Graph | Top-$`k`$ | Generation |
| KU | 43 | 4 | 39 | 0 (0.00%) | 0 (0.00%) | 36 (92.31%) | 3 (7.69%) |
| Temporal-cue proxy | 387 | 94 | 293 | 0 (0.00%) | 4 (1.37%) | 271 (92.49%) | 18 (6.14%) |
| MS, automated | 39 | 0 | 39 | 0 (0.00%) | 0 (0.00%) | 38 (97.44%) | 1 (2.56%) |
| MS, manual sensitivity | 39 | 1 | 38 | 0 (0.00%) | 0 (0.00%) | 38 (100.00%) | 0 (0.00%) |

Table 25: HaluMem category-level diagnostic attribution. Percentages in
the failure columns use incorrect examples in the corresponding row as
the denominator. The temporal-cue proxy overlaps other categories. The
last row is a manual evaluator-sensitivity view rather than a
replacement of the automated score.

#### U.4.1 Knowledge Update: Stale Memories Outrank the Latest State

HaluMem KU is overwhelmingly a top-10 coverage problem: 36/39 errors
(92.31%) occur after the relevant memories are written and structurally
reachable but before they enter the reader context. In 6/43 KU
questions, the retrieved context also contains a detectable stale value,
illustrating the competition between old and updated states.

##### Case KU-4: the latest health update is omitted while an older state is retrieved.

For halumem_medium_train_271, the question asks “What is the latest
health status of Johnson Joseph as of December 20, 2035?” The gold
answer is *Poor*, whereas the model answers *Highly Positive*. One gold
memory states that Johnson Joseph’s physical health status changed from
“Excellent” to “Poor”, and another supporting memory records chronic
fatigue. Both are written and structurally reachable, but neither enters
the top-10. Rank 4 instead contains an older update from “Good” to
“Excellent”, while higher-ranked context also contains unrelated records
about other people and topics. This is a concrete stale-state ranking
failure:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\text{latest state exists}\rightarrow\text{latest evidence ranked out}\rightarrow\text{stale state retrieved}\rightarrow\text{outdated answer}.
``` |  |

##### Case KU-5: the current update is retrieved but the answerer still abstains.

For halumem_medium_train_308, the question asks “What is Johnson
Joseph’s current monthly income as of July 18, 2039?” The gold answer is
$`\$8{,}000`$, and rank 2 explicitly states that the monthly income was
updated from $`\$7{,}000`$ to $`\$8{,}000`$. The model nevertheless
answers unknown. This is an answer-generation omission rather than a
retrieval failure.

#### U.4.2 Temporal-Cue Proxy: Time-Conditioned Retrieval and Polarity

The temporal-cue proxy contains 293 incorrect examples. Of these, 271
(92.49%) are top-10 ranking failures, 4 (1.37%) fail the structural
reachability proxy, and 18 (6.14%) remain incorrect after annotated
evidence is in the reader context. Because this subset is cue-derived
and overlaps other HaluMem categories, these numbers are used only for
diagnostic behavior, not as an official HaluMem TR score.

##### Case TR-4: all memories at the relevant date are reachable but ranked out.

For halumem_medium_train_113, the question asks “What kind of pet
ownership practices might Martin adopt after March 23, 2032?” The gold
answer is conservation-focused and ethical practices. Three gold
memories at the specified date mention conservation and ethical
considerations, conservation-focused pet ownership, and responsible
ownership and conservation efforts. All three are structurally
reachable, but none enters the top-10; the model consequently reports
insufficient information. The failure is time-conditioned reader
coverage rather than missing timestamps in memory.

##### Case TR-5: temporal/causal evidence is retrieved but the polarity conversion fails.

For halumem_medium_train_550, the question asks “Did Sarah decide to
continue consuming sugary sodas after the seminar on Aug 13, 2027?” A
rank-3 memory explicitly states that the seminar led to a decision to
“avoid sugary sodas entirely.” The model still answers unknown. Here the
evidence is available, but the answerer fails to combine the temporal
condition *after the seminar*, the causal relation between the seminar
and the decision, and the polarity conversion

|     |                                                          |     |
|-----|----------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \text{avoid entirely}\Rightarrow\text{did not continue}.
       ```                                                       |     |

#### U.4.3 Multi-Session Reasoning: Complete Support Coverage Is the Main Bottleneck

The official HaluMem Multi-hop Inference subset contains 39 examples.
Under the automated diagnostic, 38 are top-10 ranking failures and one
is labeled as an answer-generation failure. Manual inspection of that
one generation case, halumem_medium_train_2394, shows that the model
answer contains the annotated advice “expanding the studio’s creative
team”, but the longer response falls below the local token-overlap
threshold. Moving only this case to correct yields the sensitivity row
in
Table [25](#A21.T25 "Table 25 ‣ U.4 HaluMem: Category-Level Attribution ‣ Appendix U Diagnostic Analysis of Memory Failures ‣ SAGE: A Self-Evolving Agentic Graph-Memory Engine for Structure-AwareAssociative Memory"):
all 38 remaining errors are top-10 coverage failures. We keep the
automated result as the primary record and use the manual row only to
expose evaluator sensitivity.

##### Case MS-3: the motivation is retrieved but the concrete strategy is missing.

For halumem_medium_train_207, the question asks “How did Johnson’s
exploratory motivation influence his stress management strategies?” The
answer requires combining three memories: (i) his desire to explore new
skills through a part-time role; (ii) the exploratory motivation that
led him to test new approaches; and (iii) the concrete stress-management
strategies of mindfulness and scheduled downtime. The reader retrieves
only the second memory (rank 4); the first and third do not enter the
top-10. The system therefore recovers the motivation but not the
endpoint that the question asks for.

##### Case MS-4: all supporting memories are omitted and the model falls back to a plausible generic statement.

For halumem_medium_train_180, the question asks “What role did community
support play in Johnson Joseph’s career progress?” The gold answer is
“Increased guest traffic and referrals.” Three supporting memories
describe local ties producing referrals and guest numbers, a new
partnership increasing guest traffic, and Johnson’s appreciation of
community support. All three are structurally reachable but none appears
in the top-10. The model answers that community support provided
collaborative opportunities that improved his career prospects. The
response is plausible in general, but it does not recover the specific
effect supported by memory. This example illustrates how incomplete
evidence coverage can be followed by a fluent but underspecified answer
based on generic prior knowledge.

### U.5 Cross-Category Interpretation

The audits reveal different dominant failure mechanisms across memory
abilities.

##### Knowledge Update.

The latest memory is generally written, but the updated session can be
structurally inaccessible or ranked below an older state. Even when both
versions enter the context, the answerer can still select the stale
value or ignore qualifiers such as *latest*, *current*, and *as of*.
This indicates that robust long-term memory requires not only retrieval
coverage but also explicit handling of version/state validity.

##### Temporal Reasoning.

TR contains both retrieval-side and reasoning-side errors. Retrieval can
omit one or both temporal endpoints, while successful retrieval can
still be followed by incorrect duration arithmetic, relative-time
normalization, event ordering, or handling of qualifiers such as
*first*, *before*, and *after*. Therefore, increasing top-$`k`$ coverage
alone is insufficient for complete temporal-memory reasoning.

##### Multi-Session Reasoning.

MS is most sensitive to simultaneous support coverage. Relevant facts
are distributed across sessions, and a missing bridge or a single
ranked-out support can break the complete evidence set. LongMemEval
additionally shows that even complete retrieval does not eliminate
aggregation, deduplication, category-boundary, and arithmetic errors.

Overall, LongMemEval exhibits a mixed bottleneck across graph
reachability, reader ranking, and post-retrieval reasoning. HaluMem is
more strongly concentrated at reader ranking/coverage: the annotated
memories are nearly always written and structurally reachable, yet the
complete evidence set is rarely present in the top-10.

### U.6 Relation to the Paired Host-Integration Experiments

The dominant HaluMem ranking failures motivate our paired host
experiments: strong memory systems already provide mature extraction,
persistence, updating, and answer-generation machinery, while SAGE
specifically targets structure-aware memory access. Replacing the host’s
native memory-access/retrieval backend with SAGE therefore tests whether
improving this layer can add value without requiring the rest of the
host memory stack to be replaced; the exact intervention boundary is
described in
Appendix [S](#A19 "Appendix S Integrate SAGE into Multiple Memory Systems ‣ SAGE: A Self-Evolving Agentic Graph-Memory Engine for Structure-AwareAssociative Memory").

However, the diagnostic audit above and the host-integration experiments
are different evaluations. We therefore do *not* claim that the earlier
failure percentages causally explain every Native$`\rightarrow`$+SAGE
improvement. Establishing that stronger causal link would require
question-level provenance for the discordant pairs: which new evidence
entered the SAGE context, whether it was semantically required, whether
native evidence was displaced under the matched context budget, whether
a stale fact was replaced by the current fact, and whether the answer
actually used the added evidence. We consequently use the present audit
to identify failure modes and motivate the intervention, while reserving
performance attribution for the within-host matched comparisons.

|  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|
|  Configuration | HotpotQA |  | MuSiQue |  | 2Wiki |  |
|  | EM | F1 | EM | F1 | EM | F1 |
| Full end-to-end model |  |  |  |  |  |  |
|  Full SAGE, +2 rounds | 56.1 | 80.8 | 34.2 | 53.1 | 74.0 | 80.4 |
| Component ablations |  |  |  |  |  |  |
|  w/o reader-aware writer training | 52.4 | 76.7 | 30.4 | 48.3 | 68.1 | 76.8 |

|  w/o global soft addressing | 52.4 | 76.5 | 29.8 | 47.7 | 68.7 | 75.1 |

(a) reports EM and F1 (%) separately on three multi-hop QA benchmarks.
The one-shot and equal-compute controls isolate the effects of
additional optimization and writer-reader coupling, respectively. Panel
(b) further reports selector diagnostics. Best results are in bold and
runner-ups are underlined; darker cells indicate better performance.

## Limitations

SAGE treats graph memory as a dynamic substrate for writing, reading,
and self-evolution, but its effectiveness still depends on the quality
of entity extraction, relation writing, source anchoring, and reader
feedback. Our experiments show promising results across multi-hop QA,
open-domain retrieval, review-based QA, and long-term agent-memory
benchmarks, but the current system still leaves room for improvement on
memory updating, high-coverage extraction, and hallucination control in
more realistic deployments.

## Broader Impact

This work may have positive societal impact by improving the reliability
and grounding of long-horizon language agents. A structure-aware and
self-evolving memory system can help agents recover evidence chains from
fragmented cues, maintain more consistent long-term interactions, and
reduce unsupported answers in applications such as knowledge assistance,
research support, customer support, and review-based question answering.
At the same time, long-term agent memory raises important risks. If
deployed on personal or sensitive interaction histories, such systems
may store private information, infer user preferences, preserve outdated
or incorrect memories, or enable profiling and surveillance. Incorrect
graph writes or retrieval failures may also lead to confidently grounded
but wrong answers. Practical deployments should therefore use
consent-based data collection, data minimization, access control,
deletion and forgetting mechanisms, provenance tracking, auditing, and
human oversight for high-stakes use cases.

## Compute Resources

All experiments were run on a server equipped with 8 NVIDIA A100 GPUs.
The main computational cost of SAGE comes from graph-memory
construction, GFM-based graph propagation, selector regularization, and
entity-to-document projection.
Appendix [J](#A10 "Appendix J Training and Inference Complexity ‣ SAGE: A Self-Evolving Agentic Graph-Memory Engine for Structure-AwareAssociative Memory")
analyzes the training and inference complexity in terms of the number of
graph nodes $`n`$, edges $`m`$, hidden dimension $`d`$, propagation
layers $`L`$, batch size $`B`$, pseudo-queries $`M`$, and
entity-document links. In our implementation, structural features and
entity-document indices can be precomputed and cached, while edge-level
gates are computed in chunks to reduce peak GPU memory from $`O(|E|d)`$
to $`O(C_{e}d)`$ for chunk size $`C_{e}`$. The dominant inference cost
is one or a small number of query-conditioned graph propagations
followed by sparse document projection, making the reader suitable for
repeated evaluation inside the self-evolving writer-reader loop.

## Licenses and Existing Assets

This paper uses existing public benchmarks and baselines, including
NQ-Open, PopQA, HotpotQA, 2WikiMultiHopQA, MuSiQue, AmazonQA,
LongMemEval, HaluMem, BM25, Contriever, GTR, ColBERTv2, RAPTOR,
GraphRAG, G-Retriever, LightRAG, HippoRAG, HippoRAG 2, SubgraphRAG,
PropRAG, GFM-RAG, IRCoT, FLARE, and Adaptive-RAG. We cite the original
papers or repositories for these assets and use them only for research
evaluation under their stated licenses and terms of use. We do not
redistribute modified versions of the datasets beyond the preprocessing
scripts and instructions needed for reproducibility. The released code
is intended for research use and includes documentation for environment
setup, data preparation, training, and evaluation.

#### AI use statement

Generative AI tools are only used for English grammar and format
checking. No scientific claims, results, analyses, citations, or other
substantive manuscript content were changed.
````
